### [1] [Sharp bounds for non-adaptive randomized approximation of high-dimensional noisy vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00148)
*Robert J. Kunsch,Marcin Wnuk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies the complexity of approximating the finite-dimensional vector space embedding ℓ_p^m → ℓ_q^m for 2 ≤ p < q ≤ ∞ using non-adaptive randomized algorithms with up to n arbitrary linear functionals (n ≪ m), proving lower bounds that match previously known upper bounds with a joint dependence on (n, m).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The goal is to determine the fundamental limits of approximating the identity embedding between ℓ_p and ℓ_q spaces in high dimensions, where n ≪ m. Since the upper bounds were previously established, the key open question is whether these bounds are tight (optimal) — which requires proving matching lower bounds on the non-adaptive randomized approximation error with joint dependence on both n and m.

Method: The paper establishes lower bounds on the non-adaptive randomized approximation error for the finite-dimensional embedding ℓ_p^m → ℓ_q^m with 2 ≤ p < q ≤ ∞, considering algorithms that use up to n arbitrary linear functionals as information. The lower bounds demonstrate a joint dependence on (n, m) that matches previously known upper bounds.

Result: The paper proves lower bounds on the non-adaptive randomized approximation error with a joint dependence on (n, m) that matches the previously known upper bounds, thereby establishing tight (optimal) complexity results for this problem.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the complexity question for approximating ℓ_p^m → ℓ_q^m embeddings via non-adaptive randomized algorithms by proving matching lower bounds, completing the picture with the existing upper bounds and determining the exact complexity of this approximation problem.

Abstract: We study the complexity of approximating the finite-dimensional vector space embedding $\ell_p^m \hookrightarrow \ell_q^m$ for $2 \leq p < q \leq \infty$ based on non-adaptive randomized algorithms that use up to $n$ arbitrary linear functionals as information on a problem instance $x \in \mathbb{R}^m$, where $n \ll m$. We prove lower bounds on the non-adaptive randomized approximation error with a joint dependence on $(n,m)$ matching previously known upper bounds.

</details>


### [2] [Approximating the Fourier Transform from Non-equispaced Discrete Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00260)
*Daniel Potts,Laura Weidensager*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies Fourier transform approximation from finitely many samples at deterministic non-equispaced nodes, generated by transforming equispaced points through an inverse CDF of a probability density. This approach compactifies the real line (avoiding domain truncation) and yields deterministic error bounds for every frequency and in L_p, with optimized densities recovering equispaced convergence rates and reduced pre-asymptotic error constants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors depart from the standard equispaced sampling setting because it requires truncation of the space domain, which introduces errors. They seek a sampling strategy that avoids truncation while maintaining or improving convergence rates, and want deterministic error guarantees rather than statistical ones.

Method: The method uses non-equispaced sample nodes obtained by mapping equispaced points on [0,1] through the inverse cumulative distribution function (CDF) of a probability density, which compacts the real line. The analysis combines an exact aliasing identity for the midpoint rule with stationary-phase estimates for the transformed oscillatory integrals to derive deterministic error bounds.

Result: For functions with polynomial decay, an explicitly optimized density recovers the equispaced convergence rate while an additional variance parameter reduces pre-asymptotic error constants. For (sub-)exponentially decaying functions, a polynomially decaying density yields (sub-)exponential convergence rates. Numerical experiments confirm the theory and demonstrate reduced pre-asymptotic error compared with optimally scaled equispaced sampling.

Conclusion: Non-equispaced sampling via inverse CDF transformation provides a principled alternative to equispaced sampling for Fourier approximation, eliminating the need for domain truncation while achieving competitive or superior convergence rates and better pre-asymptotic behavior through density optimization.

Abstract: We study the approximation of the Fourier transform of a function from finitely many samples. Departing from the equispaced setting, we sample the function at deterministic non-equispaced nodes obtained by transforming equispaced points on $[0,1]$ through the inverse cumulative distribution function of a probability density. This change of variables compactifies the real line, so that no truncation of the space domain is necessary. Combining an exact aliasing identity for the midpoint rule with stationary-phase estimates for the transformed oscillatory integrals, we derive deterministic error bounds for every frequency and in $L_p$. For functions with polynomial decay in space and frequency, an explicitly optimized density recovers the equispaced convergence rate, while an additional variance parameter substantially reduces the pre-asymptotic error constants. For (sub-)exponentially decaying functions a polynomially decaying density yields (sub-)exponential rates. Numerical experiments confirm the theory and demonstrate the reduced pre-asymptotic error compared with optimally scaled equispaced sampling.

</details>


### [3] [Structure-Preserving Dynamical Low-Rank Approximations for Stochastic Vlasov--Poisson Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00397)
*Jianbo Cui,Carmela Scalone*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes structure-preserving dynamical low-rank methods for stochastic Vlasov-Poisson equations with transport noise, deriving continuous low-rank evolution equations that conserve mass, momentum, and energy by including fixed velocity modes. Two augmented basis-update Galerkin (BUG) integrators are developed—one using Euler-Maruyama on the Itô formulation and another using Heun on the Stratonovich formulation—and numerical experiments demonstrate conservation properties and compare the two stochastic discretization approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop low-rank approximation methods for stochastic Vlasov-Poisson equations that preserve the physical balance laws (mass, momentum, energy) of the original stochastic model, while understanding how the choice of stochastic time discretization (Itô vs. Stratonovich) interacts with the conservative low-rank framework.

Method: The authors derive continuous low-rank evolution equations and show that including fixed velocity modes associated with conserved quantities preserves balance laws. They develop two augmented basis-update Galerkin (BUG) integrators: one using Euler-Maruyama applied to the Itô formulation, and another using Heun applied directly to the Stratonovich formulation. Both use basis augmentation and conservative rank truncation to retain moment spaces and ensure robustness under rank reduction.

Result: Numerical experiments demonstrate the conservation properties of both proposed methods and compare augmentation requirements, stochastic correction terms, and momentum/energy behavior induced by the two stochastic formulations.

Conclusion: The proposed structure-preserving dynamical low-rank methods successfully maintain conservation laws for stochastic Vlasov-Poisson equations, with the choice of stochastic discretization (Itô-Euler-Maruyama vs. Stratonovich-Heun) affecting augmentation requirements and momentum/energy behavior while both retaining robustness under rank reduction.

Abstract: We propose structure-preserving dynamical low-rank methods for stochastic Vlasov--Poisson equations with transport noise. We first derive the continuous low-rank evolution equations and show that, by including fixed velocity modes associated with the conserved quantities, the low-rank dynamics inherits the mass, momentum, and energy balance laws of the original stochastic model. We then develop two augmented basis-update Galerkin (BUG) integrators based on two stochastic discretizations: an Euler--Maruyama scheme applied to the equivalent Itô formulation and a Heun scheme applied directly to the Stratonovich formulation. These two choices allow us to study how the stochastic time discretization interacts with the conservative low-rank framework. In both cases, basis augmentation and conservative rank truncation retain the relevant moment spaces and provide robustness under rank reduction. Numerical experiments demonstrate the conservation properties of the proposed methods and compare the augmentation requirements, stochastic correction terms, and momentum and energy behavior induced by the two stochastic formulations.

</details>


### [4] [From Pólya's Conditions to a Complete Characterization of the $L^2$ Convergence of Hyperinterpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00525)
*Congpei An,Xiannan Hu,Xiaoming Yuan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper identifies necessary and sufficient conditions for L² convergence of hyperinterpolation (first introduced by Sloan in 1995): the L¹-L² Marcinkiewicz-Zygmund condition combined with the asymptotic functional approximation property for polynomials. It proves the optimal MZ constant equals the operator norm of the hyperinterpolation operator with a Banach space duality interpretation, and shows Pólya's classical quadrature convergence conditions are insufficient for hyperinterpolation convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The necessary and sufficient conditions for L² convergence of hyperinterpolation have remained an open problem since its introduction by Sloan in 1995. This paper aims to resolve this long-standing open question and clarify the fundamental distinction between convergence of quadrature formulas and hyperinterpolation operators.

Method: The authors identify the L¹-L² Marcinkiewicz-Zygmund condition together with the asymptotic functional approximation property for polynomials as the answer. They prove the optimal MZ constant coincides with the operator norm of the hyperinterpolation operator via Banach space duality, and provide an explicit construction to demonstrate that Pólya's classical conditions are insufficient for hyperinterpolation convergence.

Result: The paper proves that the L¹-L² MZ condition plus the asymptotic functional approximation property are necessary and sufficient for L² convergence of hyperinterpolation. It shows Pólya's conditions fail to guarantee hyperinterpolation convergence, establishing a strict logical hierarchy for stability and accuracy conditions governing quadrature versus hyperinterpolation convergence.

Conclusion: The open problem from 1995 is resolved. The results reveal a fundamental distinction between the convergence of linear functionals (quadrature) and linear operators (hyperinterpolation), formalized through a strict logical hierarchy of stability and accuracy conditions.

Abstract: It has remained open to identify the necessary and sufficient conditions for the $L^2$ convergence of hyperinterpolation since it was introduced by Sloan in 1995. We show that the $L^1$-$L^2$ Marcinkiewicz-Zygmund (MZ) condition, together with the asymptotic functional approximation property for polynomials, is the answer. We further prove that the optimal $L^1$-$L^2$ MZ constant coincides with the operator norm of the hyperinterpolation operator, and it admits a natural Banach space duality interpretation. With an explicit construction, we also show that Pólya's classical conditions for quadrature convergence are not sufficient for the $L^2$ convergence of hyperinterpolation. This reveals a fundamental distinction between the convergence of linear functionals (quadrature formulas) and that of linear operators (hyperinterpolation operators). We establish a strict logical hierarchy for the stability and accuracy conditions governing the convergence of quadrature and hyperinterpolation.

</details>


### [5] [A semi-implicit double-point Material Point Method for both free-surface flow and seepage in deformable porous media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00578)
*Mian Xie,Pedro Navas,Susana López-Querol*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A new semi-implicit, two-phase, double-point MPM formulation for soil-water interaction is presented, advancing the water phase implicitly while keeping soil explicit, enabling stable large-deformation problems involving rapid seepage, free-surface flows, and soil-water coupling under geotechnical hazards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable stable, efficient numerical simulation of intense soil-water interaction problems involving rapid seepage, free-surface motion, and large deformation, which pose challenges for existing explicit time-integration MPM approaches.

Method: A semi-implicit two-phase double-point MPM with implicit water advancement and explicit soil advancement; non-linear Darcy's law for high-Reynolds-number interphase drag implemented within an incremental fractional step framework without enlarging the implicit solve; hyperelastic constitutive treatment of slightly compressible viscous water; a new velocity stabilisation scheme; nodal-based free-surface detection suited for higher-order spline functions with smooth porosity-permeability transitions; and a critical-state soil model (NorSand).

Result: Validated against laboratory benchmarks including pure-water dam break, dam-break seepage through porous barrier, two granular-collapse tsunami experiments, and dam-break wave over movable granular bed, showing accurate and stable free-surface evolution, pressure time histories, seepage fronts, and wave-gauge records. NorSand further improves granular flow kinematics reproduction.

Conclusion: The proposed formulation is a reliable and computationally efficient tool for geotechnical hazards involving intense soil-water coupling, seepage, sediment transport, and free water.

Abstract: A new semi-implicit, two-phase, double-point formulation of the Material Point Method (MPM) for soil-water interaction with seepage and free-surface flows under large deformation is presented in this paper. The approach advances the water phase implicitly while keeping the soil phase explicit, enabling stable, efficient time integration in problems that involve rapid seepage and strong free-surface motion. The proposed framework models high-Reynolds-number interphase drag through a non-linear Darcy's law implemented for the first time within an incremental fractional step MPM formulation without enlarging the implicit solve. This methodology also enhances the numerical stability for fast flows and wave breaking via a hyperelastic constitutive treatment of slightly compressible viscous water, and mitigates spurious oscillations through a new stabilisation approach for the velocity. Robustness of soil-water interface is achieved by combining nodal-based, free-surface detection, suited for higher-order spline functions with smooth porosity-permeability transitions that avoid constitutive divergence at sharp material boundaries. Validation against laboratory benchmark cases reported in the literature, including pure-water dam break, dam-break seepage through a porous barrier, two granular-collapse tsunami experiments, and a dam-break wave over a movable granular bed, shows accurate and stable free-surface evolution, pressure time histories, seepage fronts, and wave-gauge records. Using an advanced critical-state soil model (NorSand) further improves the reproduction of granular flow kinematics. The results demonstrate that the proposed formulation is a reliable and computationally efficient tool for geotechnical hazards involving intense soil-water coupling, seepage, sediment transport and free water.

</details>


### [6] [Study of Landslides through a Stabilised Semi-Implicit Material Point Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00592)
*Mian Xie,Pedro Navas,Susana López-Querol*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new semi-implicit two-phase double-point material point method (MPM) where soil and water are modeled with two distinct sets of material points, stabilized with a novel approach and coupled with the Nor-Sand constitutive model. Landslide simulations show the double-point approach yields more reliable results than conventional single-point methods, with acceptable additional computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the accuracy of simulating large-deformation geotechnical problems such as landslides by addressing limitations of conventional single-point material point methods in modeling two-phase soil-water interactions.

Method: A semi-implicit two-phase double-point material point method is proposed, using two separate sets of material points for soil and water phases, stabilized with a novel technique, and incorporating the Nor-Sand constitutive model for realistic soil behavior.

Result: The double-point formulation produced significantly different but more reliable results for landslide cases compared to the single-point approach. The extra computational cost from additional water material points was found to be acceptable.

Conclusion: Two sets of material points are recommended for large deformation geotechnical problems due to improved reliability with acceptable computational overhead.

Abstract: In this research, a new semi-implicit two-phase double-point material point method is proposed, in which the soil and water phases are modelled using two distinct sets of material points, both being stabilised with a novel approach. The Nor-Sand constitutive model is implemented to simulate more realistic soil behaviour. Some landslide numerical examples are presented to investigate the performance of the proposed method and highlight the importance of using the double-point approach. The formulation with two sets of material points shows significantly different but more reliable results in the cases of landslides, compared with the conventional single-point approach. Furthermore, this research shows that the additional computational cost given by the additional water material points is acceptable. Therefore, it is recommended to use two sets of material points for some large deformation geotechnical problems.

</details>


### [7] [Spectral Algorithms for 3-Wave Kinetic and $C_{12}$ Quantum Boltzmann Equations with General Resonance Manifolds in $\mathbb{R}^d$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00603)
*Thanh Trung Le,Minh-Binh Tran*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops spectral algorithms for multidimensional 3-wave kinetic equations and C12 quantum Boltzmann equations with general polynomial dispersion relations, using truncated Fourier representations of the Dirac delta to handle resonance constraints. Two methods are proposed: a direct spectral method and a faster FFT-based approach. Numerical experiments validate both methods and explore cascade dynamics, filtering strategies, and the influence of dispersion relations and spatial dimension.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The principal numerical difficulty arises from the resonance constraint, which is supported on a nonlinear manifold in wave-vector space. Efficient and accurate spectral methods are needed to simulate multidimensional kinetic equations with general polynomial dispersion relations, going beyond existing numerical schemes for 3-wave kinetic equations.

Method: The Dirac distribution is approximated by a truncated Fourier representation, enabling two spectral discretizations of the collision operator: (1) a direct spectral method with complexity O(L(2N)^{3d}), and (2) a fast method using multidimensional FFTs reducing complexity to O(L(2N)^{2d} log(2N)). The 2/3-rule and exponential spectral filtering are combined to suppress unresolved high-frequency modes.

Result: Numerical tests in two and three dimensions show excellent agreement between the two methods, with the fast algorithm providing substantial computational savings. Simulations capture gain-loss dynamics of the C12 quantum Boltzmann equation for rapidly and algebraically decaying initial data. For the 3-wave kinetic equation, strong oscillations and rapid spectral broadening are observed, providing numerical evidence of an energy cascade toward high frequencies. The dispersion relation and spatial dimension strongly influence transient resonant dynamics.

Conclusion: The proposed spectral algorithms are effective for simulating multidimensional 3-wave and C12 quantum Boltzmann equations, offering significant computational improvements while accurately capturing resonance-driven dynamics, cascades, and the influence of dispersion relations and dimensionality.

Abstract: Following recent developments in numerical schemes for 3-wave kinetic equations [2, 7, 42, 44, 43], we develop spectral algorithms for multidimensional 3-wave kinetic equations and $C_{12}$ quantum Boltzmann equations with general polynomial dispersion relations. The principal numerical difficulty arises from the resonance constraint, supported on a nonlinear manifold in wave-vector space. We approximate the Dirac distribution by a truncated Fourier representation and derive two spectral discretizations of the collision operator. The first is a direct spectral method with complexity $\mathcal{O}\big(L(2N)^{3d}\big)$, while the second exploits multidimensional FFTs to reduce the complexity to $\mathcal{O}\big(L(2N)^{2d}\log(2N)\big)$. Numerical tests show excellent agreement between the two methods, with the fast algorithm providing substantial computational savings. To suppress unresolved high-frequency modes, we combine the classical $2/3$-rule with exponential spectral filtering. Simulations in two and three dimensions capture the gain--loss dynamics of the $C_{12}$ quantum Boltzmann equation for both rapidly and algebraically decaying initial data. For the 3-wave kinetic equation, the computations exhibit strong oscillations and rapid spectral broadening, providing numerical evidence of an apparent energy cascade toward high frequencies. The results also show that the dispersion relation and spatial dimension strongly influence the transient resonant dynamics.

</details>


### [8] [Variational Inference Using a Differentiable Multigrid Linear Solver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00760)
*Andrés Ramírez,Philipp Haim,David Föger,Torsten A. Enßlin,Philipp Frank,Philipp Gschwandtner,Dominik Jüstel,Philipp Mertsch,Vo Hong Minh Phan,Laurin Söding,Hanieh Zandinejad,Ralf Kissmann*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: DMGS is a differentiable multigrid solver for steady-state diffusion-absorption PDEs, implemented in C++ with JAX/Python bindings, that provides efficient Jacobian-vector and vector-Jacobian products via analytically derived adjoint operations. It is validated on a 3D diffuse radiative transfer inverse problem in tissue imaging, achieving a reduced chi-squared of 1.1 with lower peak memory than a JAX-native alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gradient-based Bayesian inference requires efficient Jacobian and adjoint-Jacobian access for high-dimensional forward models. While multigrid solvers are near-optimal for elliptic PDEs, they are rarely compatible with automatic differentiation (AD), limiting their use in probabilistic programming frameworks.

Method: The authors derive the adjoint operations analytically through the complete multigrid hierarchy, implement the solver (DMGS) in C++, and interface it with JAX. This enables efficient Jacobian-vector products (JVPs) and vector-Jacobian products (VJPs) for variational inference within the NIFTy framework.

Result: The solver reconstructs an effective radiative source from Monte Carlo-simulated 3D tissue data with a reduced chi-squared of 1.1, generalizes to 32 validation datasets, and shows comparable runtime with consistently lower peak memory versus a JAX-native multigrid implementation, with modest reverse-mode overhead.

Conclusion: Differentiable multigrid solvers are practical and efficient building blocks for variational inference in PDE-constrained inverse problems, bridging the gap between optimal-complexity numerical solvers and modern AD-based probabilistic programming.

Abstract: Gradient-based Bayesian inference methods require efficient access to Jacobian and adjoint-Jacobian operators of high-dimensional forward models. While multigrid solvers provide near-optimal complexity for elliptic partial differential equations, they are rarely available in forms compatible with automatic differentiation (AD). We develop a differentiable multigrid solver for steady-state diffusion-absorption problems and derive its adjoint operations analytically through the full multigrid hierarchy. The resulting solver, DMGS, is implemented in C++ and interfaced with JAX to provide efficient Jacobian-vector and vector-Jacobian products for variational inference in the NIFTy framework. We validate the approach on a 3D inverse problem involving diffuse radiative transfer in tissue, reconstructing an effective radiative source from Monte Carlo-simulated data. The reconstruction reproduces the data at a reduced chi-squared of 1.1 and generalizes to 32 independent validation datasets. Benchmarks against a JAX-native multigrid implementation show comparable runtimes and consistently lower peak memory for the hand-derived adjoint, with modest reverse-mode overhead. These results establish differentiable multigrid solvers as practical building blocks for variational inference in PDE-constrained problems.

</details>


### [9] [A Helmholtz-Leray projection method with variational multiscale stabilization for the Navier-Stokes equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00843)
*Biswajit Khara,Suresh Murugaiyan,Makrand Khanwale,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper presents a residual-based variational multiscale (VMS) stabilization of an incremental Helmholtz-Leray projection method for incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, which replaces the coupled saddle-point problem with a nonlinear velocity predictor, pressure Poisson equation, and velocity projection. The method applies multiscale decomposition only to the predicted velocity, yielding SUPG-like and PSPG-like stabilization, with formal error analysis showing second-order temporal accuracy and numerical results matching reference data while reducing computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Galerkin finite element formulation of incompressible Navier-Stokes equations faces two principal challenges: maintaining stable velocity-pressure coupling and controlling instability in advection-dominated regimes. Additionally, the monolithic formulation produces a coupled nonlinear saddle-point problem that is computationally expensive to solve. The authors aim to address these challenges with an efficient projection-based approach.

Method: The method is a residual-based VMS stabilization of an incremental Helmholtz-Leray projection method that decomposes the problem into three subproblems: a nonlinear velocity predictor, a pressure Poisson equation, and a velocity projection. The multiscale decomposition is applied only to the predicted velocity (not to pressure or the corrected weakly divergence-free velocity), and the modeled velocity fine scale contributes consistently to all three subproblems, introducing SUPG-like stabilization in the momentum predictor and PSPG-like residual contribution in the pressure Poisson equation. A formal error decomposition separates BDF2 time-discretization, projection-splitting, and spatial-VMS errors.

Result: Numerical results for manufactured solutions, lid-driven cavity flow, flow past a cylinder, and Taylor-Green vortex agree closely with established reference data. Comparisons with monolithic VMS show that omitting the pressure fine scale reduces drag overprediction and excess modeled dissipation, at the cost of increased divergence error. In Taylor-Green tests, the projection formulation reduces average solution time per step by factors of approximately 1.3x to 2.7x under identical solver settings, and the combined velocity error estimate achieves second-order temporal accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed projection-based VMS method effectively addresses the challenges of the monolithic Galerkin formulation by replacing the coupled saddle-point problem with a more tractable three-step procedure, while maintaining accuracy through consistent fine-scale modeling and second-order temporal convergence. The approach offers computational efficiency gains (1.3x-2.7x speedup) and improved physical accuracy (reduced drag overprediction and dissipation), with the tradeoff of increased divergence error.

Abstract: The Galerkin finite element formulation of the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations presents two principal challenges: maintaining stable velocity-pressure coupling and controlling instability in advection-dominated regimes. Moreover, the monolithic formulation produces a coupled nonlinear saddle-point problem. In this work, we present a residual-based variational multiscale (VMS) stabilization of an incremental Helmholtz-Leray projection method that replaces this coupled saddle-point problem with a nonlinear velocity predictor, a pressure Poisson equation, and a velocity projection. The multiscale decomposition is applied only to the predicted velocity; neither the pressure nor the corrected, weakly divergence-free velocity is decomposed into coarse and fine scales. The modeled velocity fine scale contributes consistently to all three subproblems, introducing SUPG-like stabilization in the momentum predictor and a PSPG-like residual contribution in the pressure Poisson equation. We provide a formal error decomposition that separates the BDF2 time-discretization, projection-splitting, and spatial-VMS errors and, under stated stability and spatial-approximation assumptions, yields a combined velocity error estimate with second-order temporal accuracy. Numerical results for manufactured solutions, lid-driven cavity flow, flow past a cylinder, and the Taylor-Green vortex agree closely with established reference data. Comparisons with monolithic VMS indicate that omitting the pressure fine scale reduces drag overprediction and excess modeled dissipation, at the cost of increased divergence error. In the Taylor-Green tests, the projection formulation also reduces the average solution time per step by factors ranging from approximately $1.3\times$ to $2.7\times$ under identical solver settings.

</details>


### [10] [Randomized iterative methods with Polyak step-size for solving generalized absolute value equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00952)
*Jiayun Chen,Qiye Zhang,Deren Han,Jiaxin Xie*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper incorporates the Polyak step-size into randomized iterative methods for solving generalized absolute value equations. Unlike the classical Polyak step-size for deterministic optimization, the authors adopt it in a stochastic setting with dynamically updating objective functions, requiring dedicated convergence analysis. They prove linear convergence in expectation and demonstrate substantial computational improvements over constant step-size methods in numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Randomized iterative methods for solving generalized absolute value equations typically use constant step-sizes, which may be suboptimal. The Polyak step-size has been effective in deterministic optimization but has not been systematically incorporated into randomized iterative methods, where the objective function changes dynamically at each step, making direct application non-trivial.

Method: The authors incorporate the Polyak step-size into a stochastic iterative framework for solving generalized absolute value equations. The key novelty is adapting the Polyak step-size to a setting where the objective function updates dynamically at each iteration (unlike the classical setting with fixed objectives), which requires a dedicated convergence analysis distinct from conventional Polyak schemes.

Result: The proposed method achieves linear convergence in expectation, rigorously established through convergence analysis. Numerical experiments show that incorporating the Polyak step-size substantially improves computational performance compared to randomized iterative methods using constant step-sizes.

Conclusion: The Polyak step-size can be effectively generalized to stochastic iterative settings with dynamically changing objective functions, yielding faster convergence for solving generalized absolute value equations. The linear convergence guarantee combined with significant numerical performance gains validates the approach.

Abstract: In this paper, we systematically incorporate the Polyak step-size into the randomized iterative method to improve its efficiency for solving generalized absolute value equations. In particular, we adopt the Polyak step-size within a stochastic iterative setting where the objective function updates dynamically at every step, unlike the classical Polyak step-size designed for deterministic optimization with fixed objective functions. Consequently, this novel implementation differs from the conventional Polyak scheme and demands a dedicated convergence analysis. We rigorously analyze the convergence properties of the proposed method and establish its linear convergence in expectation. Numerical experiments demonstrate that the incorporation of the Polyak step-size substantially improves the computational performance of randomized iterative methods with constant step-sizes.

</details>


### [11] [Self-similar structure of non-isothermal variable-density mushy Stefan problems and an improved low-Mach enthalpy method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00960)
*Yavkreet Swami,Amneet Pal Singh Bhalla*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies the convergence properties of the enthalpy method for phase-change problems, showing that as the artificial mushy zone thickness (ΔT) approaches zero, the solution converges to the classical Stefan problem—but this behavior depends on density ratios. The authors formulate a finite-ΔT mushy Stefan problem, exploit its self-similar structure to reduce it to a boundary-value problem, and use the analysis to improve their low-Mach enthalpy method for high-density-ratio PCMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The enthalpy method is widely used for simulating melting/solidification, and it is generally assumed that its solutions converge to the classical Stefan problem as the artificial mushy region shrinks (ΔT→0). However, this assumption has only been verified under the simplifying assumption of equal solid and liquid densities, and its validity for density-ratio phase-change problems is unknown.

Method: The authors formulate and analyze the finite-ΔT mushy Stefan problem solved by the enthalpy method. They show this problem has a self-similar structure that reduces the governing PDEs to a boundary-value problem with two unknown parameters. This theoretical framework is then used to improve their previously developed low-Mach enthalpy method for enhanced stability and accuracy at high density ratios.

Result: The theoretical analysis confirms/reveals the convergence behavior of the enthalpy method for both low- and high-density-ratio phase-change problems. It enables improvements to the low-Mach enthalpy method, enhancing stability and accuracy as the density ratio increases from O(1) to O(3).

Conclusion: The enthalpy method's convergence to the classical Stefan problem is systematically validated and analyzed via the finite-ΔT mushy Stefan problem, and the resulting theoretical insights lead to a more robust and accurate enthalpy method for realistic density-ratio phase-change simulations.

Abstract: The enthalpy method was introduced in the late 1970s to simulate phase-change problems on fixed grids without explicitly tracking the moving phase-change front. It remains one of the most widely used approaches in academic and commercial software for the simulation of industrial melting and solidification processes. For pure phase-change materials (PCMs) that melt or solidify at a single temperature, the enthalpy method introduces an artificial mushy region bounded by the solidus temperature, $T^{\rm sol}$, and the liquidus temperature, $T^{\rm liq}$. As the numerical parameter $ΔT=T^{\rm liq}- T^{\rm sol}$ approaches zero, the solution obtained with the enthalpy method is generally assumed to converge to that of the classical Stefan problem, in which the phase-change front is infinitesimally thin. This assumption is largely based on benchmark studies performed under the simplifying assumption of equal solid and liquid densities. In this work, we systematically investigate the accuracy and spatio-temporal convergence properties of the enthalpy method for both low- and high-density-ratio phase-change problems. Because the limiting behavior $ΔT\rightarrow0$ is difficult to realize numerically, owing to the diminishing thickness of the mushy region, we formulate and analyze the finite-$ΔT$ mushy Stefan problem solved by the enthalpy method. We show that this problem possesses a self-similar structure that reduces the governing equations to a boundary-value problem involving two unknown parameters. The theoretical analysis also enables improvements to our previously developed low-Mach enthalpy method, enhancing its stability and accuracy as the density ratio between the two phases increases from $\mathcal{O}(1)$ to $\mathcal{O}(3)$.

</details>


### [12] [An Integral-Based Framework for Preconditioning $f(A)b$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01003)
*Gustavo Ramirez-Hidalgo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified framework for preconditioning matrix function-vector products f(A)b, based on Cauchy integral representation, with two complementary approaches: rational shift-and-invert preconditioning stabilized via Double Modified Gram-Schmidt reorthogonalization, and a matrix-free polynomial approach using Schur decomposition and exact LR-deflation. Tested on 2D Laplacian (exp) and Wilson-Dirac operator (sign).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing f(A)b for large sparse matrices is a critical bottleneck, and standard Krylov methods stagnate when the matrix has unfavorable spectral distributions. There is a need for effective preconditioning strategies, particularly for applications involving many different vectors b_i.

Method: The framework is based on the Cauchy integral representation of matrix functions and exploits shift-invariance to decouple preconditioner evaluation from Krylov subspace generation. Two directions are developed: (1) rational shift-and-invert preconditioning with closed-form extraction stabilized via Double Modified Gram-Schmidt reorthogonalization to eliminate phantom poles; (2) a matrix-free polynomial approach with Schur decomposition of the projected Hessenberg matrix for stable numerical quadrature, plus exact LR-deflation targeting critical low modes. Asymptotic stability and proximity to singularity are analyzed.

Result: Numerical experiments demonstrate efficiency on the 2D Laplacian with f=exp and a highly ill-conditioned Wilson-Dirac operator from lattice QCD with f=sign. The framework is general and applicable to any f, being particularly beneficial for many different vectors b_i.

Conclusion: The proposed unified preconditioning framework resolves the trade-off between spectral compression and finite-precision instability in rational approaches, and provides numerically stable polynomial alternatives, making it broadly applicable for accelerating f(A)b computations in challenging spectral regimes.

Abstract: The computation of the action of a matrix function on a vector, $f(A)b$, is a major computational bottleneck for large, sparse matrices, particularly when unfavorable spectral distributions cause standard Krylov subspace methods to stagnate. In this work, we propose a unified framework for preconditioning $f(A)b$ based on the Cauchy integral representation of the matrix function. By exploiting shift-invariance properties, we decouple the preconditioner evaluation from the Krylov subspace generation. We develop this framework in two distinct directions. First, for rational shift-and-invert preconditioning, we resolve a fundamental trade-off between optimal spectral compression and finite-precision instability. We achieve this by formulating a closed-form extraction stabilized via Double Modified Gram-Schmidt reorthogonalization, which eliminates the formation of spurious phantom poles. Second, we present a matrix-free polynomial approach. To ensure numerical stability, we isolate the continuous numerical quadrature step using a Schur decomposition of the projected Hessenberg matrix. To further stabilize the integration near contour singularities and accelerate overall convergence, we incorporate an exact LR-deflation scheme targeting the critical low modes of the preconditioned operator. We analyze the asymptotic stability and proximity to singularity of these methods, and present numerical experiments demonstrating their efficiency on the 2D Laplacian with $f=\textrm{exp}$, and a highly ill-conditioned Wilson-Dirac operator from lattice quantum chromodynamics with $f=\textrm{sign}$, although the framework can be in principle used with any $f$ and it is particularly beneficial when applying $f(A)b_{i}$ with many different vectors $b_{i}$.

</details>


### [13] [A Novel Bijective Angle and Volume-preservation Balanced Parameterization for $n$-dimensional Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01073)
*Tiexiang Li,Wen-Wei Lin,Zhong-Heng Tan,Xiao Wan,Junxin Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A unified framework for balanced and bijective parameterizations of n-dimensional manifolds, combining conformal and volume-preserving energies with a cotangent Laplacian-type gradient representation for sparse, dimension-independent computation, with bijectivity enforced via signed simplex Jacobians and orientation-preserving barriers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a unified approach that handles both conformal (anisotropy) and volume-preserving (volumetric distortion) aspects of manifold parameterization across arbitrary dimensions, addressing the lack of a general framework that enforces bijectivity while controlling both types of distortion.

Method: Combines conformal and volume-preserving energies (both nonnegative with characterized zero-energy mappings) formulated on oriented simplicial manifolds. All gradients share a unified cotangent Laplacian-type representation for sparse computation. Bijectivity is enforced through signed simplex Jacobians, feasibility restoration, and a strictly orientation-preserving logarithmic barrier. The framework applies uniformly to spherical boundary parameterizations and n-manifolds onto ball-like domains.

Result: A dimension-independent, sparse computational framework that unifies conformal and volume-preserving parameterization energies, with guaranteed bijectivity through orientation-preserving constraints.

Conclusion: The framework provides a general, dimension-independent solution for balanced and bijective parameterizations spanning spherical boundary cases and ball-like canonical domains across n-dimensional manifolds.

Abstract: We propose a unified framework for balanced and bijective parameterizations of $n$-dimensional manifolds. The proposed energy combines conformal and volume-preserving terms to control both local anisotropy and volumetric distortion. At the continuous level, both energies are nonnegative and their zero-energy mappings are characterized. At the discrete level, the conformal, volume-preserving, and logarithmic barrier energies are formulated on oriented simplicial manifolds. A key result is that all their gradients admit a unified cotangent Laplacian-type representation, enabling sparse and dimension-independent computation. Bijectivity is enforced through signed simplex Jacobians, feasibility restoration, and a strictly orientation-preserving logarithmic barrier. The framework applies uniformly to spherical boundary parameterizations and parameterizations of discrete $n$-manifolds onto ball-like canonical domains.

</details>

<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of nonlinear radiation thermal transfer in composite structures with temperature-dependent properties is critical for high-temperature material applications, yet existing approaches face challenges in accuracy and computational efficiency.

Method: The study establishes a high-accuracy multi-scale computational model with novel high-order correction terms that preserve local heat quantity balance. It derives explicit convergence rates for the high-order multi-scale solutions and develops an efficient multi-scale algorithm with off-line and on-line computation stages, along with corresponding convergence analysis.

Result: Two- and three-dimensional numerical examples validate the approach, demonstrating exceptional numerical accuracy and reduced computational cost in both storage requirements and computational time compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-scale approach offers competitive advantages for high-fidelity simulation of nonlinear thermal radiation in composite structures, balancing high accuracy with computational efficiency.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of the nonlinear radiation thermal transfer in composite structures with temperature-dependent properties is significant in high-temperature applications of the materials. This study establishes a high-accuracy multi-scale computational model incorporating novel high-order correction terms for the high-fidelity simulation of nonlinear thermal radiation in composite structures, enabling local balance preserving of heat quantity. Moreover, an explicit convergence rate is also derived for the resulting high-order multi-scale solutions. Furthermore, an efficient multi-scale algorithm consisting of off-line and on-line computation stages is developed for high-accuracy simulation of nonlinear thermal radiation behavior in composite structures, and corresponding convergence analysis is also obtained. Two- and three-dimensional numerical examples are presented to validate the competitive advantages of the proposed multi-scale approach, not only exceptional numerical accuracy, but also reduced computational cost in both storage requirements and computational time.

</details>


### [15] [Generalized Modulo Hysteresis Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01270)
*Matthias Beckmann,Jürgen Jeschke*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces generalized modulo encoders that extend the modulo hysteresis model by incorporating a general transition function and allowing folding times to depend on transition nonidealities, with rigorous analysis of operator properties, uniqueness conditions for bandlimited signal recovery, and a mathematically guaranteed recovery algorithm validated by numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard modulo sampling (unlimited sensing) and its modulo hysteresis extension have limitations in how they model the folding behavior, particularly regarding the idealistic assumptions about the folding transition. The authors aim to overcome these shortcomings by proposing a more flexible encoding framework.

Method: The paper proposes generalized modulo encoders that incorporate a general transition function and allow folding times to depend on the nonideality of the folding transition. They rigorously analyze the mathematical properties of the associated operators and derive conditions for unique determination of bandlimited signals from generalized modulo samples. A recovery algorithm with mathematical guarantees is also developed.

Result: The authors derive conditions under which a bandlimited signal is uniquely determined by its generalized modulo samples and propose a recovery algorithm backed by mathematical guarantees, with numerical experiments illustrating the theoretical results.

Conclusion: Generalized modulo encoders provide a more flexible and realistic framework for folding high dynamic range signals, with theoretical guarantees for signal recovery from such encodings.

Abstract: Unlimited sensing and its extension via modulo hysteresis provide an efficient encoding scheme for high dynamic range signals by folding the signal's amplitude into the range of the analog-to-digital converter prior to sampling. Motivated by shortcomings of the modulo hysteresis model, in this work we introduce generalized modulo encoders that flexibly design the folding by incorporating a general transition function and allowing the folding times to depend on the nonideality of the folding transition. We rigorously study mathematical properties of the respective operators and derive conditions under which a bandlimited signal is uniquely determined by its generalized modulo samples. Finally, we propose a recovery algorithm backed by mathematical guarantees and illustrate our theoretical results through numerical experiments.

</details>


### [16] [On a Class of Block-Regularized Preconditioned Iterative Methods for Indefinite Least-Squares Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01273)
*Pinki Khatun*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper introduces a block-regularized splitting (BRS) framework for efficiently solving indefinite least-squares (ILS) problems, including a BRS iterative method and preconditioner, plus a relaxed variant (RBRS) with improved spectral properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Indefinite least-squares (ILS) problems are computationally challenging, and existing methods often struggle with efficiency for large-scale problems. The authors aim to develop a more effective iterative solver and preconditioner framework.

Method: The authors propose a block-regularized splitting (BRS) framework combining block-wise regularization with matrix splitting to create a BRS iterative method and a BRS preconditioner. They also introduce a relaxed BRS (RBRS) preconditioner to further improve spectral properties and accelerate Krylov subspace convergence.

Result: Convergence of the BRS method is rigorously analyzed with established spectral bounds. Numerical experiments on dense and sparse problems show BRS and RBRS preconditioners consistently outperform existing approaches in iteration count, computational time, and overall efficiency.

Conclusion: The BRS/RBRS framework is effective and robust for solving large-scale ILS problems, offering significant performance improvements over existing preconditioning techniques.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel block-regularized splitting (BRS) framework for the efficient solution of indefinite least-squares (ILS) problems. Based on a block-wise regularization strategy incorporated into a matrix splitting scheme, we develop a BRS iterative method together with an effective BRS preconditioner. The convergence of the proposed iterative method is rigorously analyzed, and spectral bounds for the BRS-preconditioned matrix are established. To further enhance computational performance, we introduce a relaxed BRS (RBRS) preconditioner, which provides improved spectral properties and significantly accelerates the convergence of Krylov subspace methods. Extensive numerical experiments on both dense and sparse test problems demonstrate that the proposed BRS and RBRS preconditioners consistently outperform existing approaches in terms of iteration count, computational time, and overall efficiency. These results highlight the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed block-regularized splitting framework for solving large-scale ILS problems.

</details>


### [17] [High-order WENO-based semi-implicit Newton-type fast sweeping methods for static Hamilton-Jacobi equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01304)
*Yuan Liu,Jianliang Qian*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends the Newton-type fast sweeping method for the generalized Eikonal equation to high-order accuracy (fifth, seventh, and ninth order) using WENO approximations within a semi-implicit Gauss-Seidel Lax-Friedrichs framework, with numerical verification in 2D and 3D.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the accuracy of existing Newton-type semi-implicit fast sweeping methods for solving the generalized Eikonal equation in moving fluid wave propagation, which previously had limited order of accuracy.

Method: The method builds on the Newton-type framework of Li and Qian (2020) with line-wise updates and a tridiagonal diagonally dominant Jacobian, extending the local solver to fifth-, seventh-, and ninth-order accuracy by incorporating high-order WENO spatial derivative approximations into the numerical Hamiltonian. Three alternating sweeping strategies (column-wise, row-wise, column-row-wise) are used within the Gauss-Seidel iteration.

Result: Numerical examples in both two and three spatial dimensions demonstrate the efficiency and accuracy of the proposed high-order schemes.

Conclusion: The proposed high-order WENO-based semi-implicit Newton-type Gauss-Seidel Lax-Friedrichs fast sweeping methods are efficient and accurate for solving the generalized Eikonal equation, validated across multiple spatial dimensions.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose high-order weighted essentially non-oscillatory (WENO)-based semi-implicit Newton-type Gauss-Seidel Lax-Friedrichs fast sweeping methods for solving the generalized Eikonal equation arising in wave propagation through a moving fluid. Building upon the Newton-type framework of Li and Qian (2020), which updates the solution line-wise using Newton's method with a tridiagonal and strictly diagonally dominant Jacobian, we extend the local solver to fifth-, seventh-, and ninth-order accuracy by incorporating high-order WENO approximations of the spatial derivatives into the numerical Hamiltonian. Three alternating sweeping strategies, namely column-wise, row-wise, and column-row-wise, are considered within the Gauss-Seidel iteration framework. Numerical examples in both two and three spatial dimensions demonstrate the efficiency and accuracy of the proposed schemes.

</details>


### [18] [Control variates with neural surrogates for uncertainty quantification in kinetic equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01360)
*Wei Chen,Giacomo Dimarco,Lorenzo Pareschi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes multifidelity control variate estimators where the low-fidelity model is replaced by a neural surrogate for uncertainty quantification in kinetic equations. The authors prove the estimator remains unbiased, bound the optimal variance change by the neural approximation error, extend analysis to multiple control variates and an asymptotic-preserving hierarchy, and validate with numerical tests using micro-macro neural surrogates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification for kinetic equations (Boltzmann, Landau, collisional models) requires repeated high-dimensional simulations that are computationally prohibitive. Multifidelity control variates can alleviate this by coupling few high-fidelity simulations with many low-fidelity evaluations, but prior work assumed classical numerical evaluation of reduced models, whereas neural surrogates offer a potentially more efficient alternative that needs theoretical analysis.

Method: The authors replace the exact low-fidelity model in a multifidelity control variate framework with a neural surrogate, and prove that (1) the estimator remains unbiased, (2) the optimal variance deviation from the exact case is bounded by the error between the exact low-fidelity observable and its neural approximation. They combine these results with residual stability estimates for inhomogeneous Fokker-Planck and Bhatnagar-Gross-Krook surrogates, extend to multiple control variates, and analyze an asymptotic-preserving (AP) hierarchy including the limiting Euler observable.

Result: The optimal hierarchical variance converges to the variance of the limiting Euler control in the fluid limit, while the intermediate kinetic correction contribution vanishes. Numerical tests with micro-macro neural surrogates confirm the predicted variance stability and the behavior of the AP hierarchy.

Conclusion: Neural surrogates can safely replace classical reduced models in multifidelity control variate estimators for kinetic equations while preserving unbiasedness and controlling variance degradation through the neural approximation error, with a well-understood asymptotic-preserving hierarchy behavior in the fluid limit.

Abstract: Efficient uncertainty quantification for kinetic equations with random inputs is challenging because it requires repeated simulations of high-dimensional models, such as the Boltzmann, Landau, and related collisional equations, whose computational cost can quickly become prohibitive. Multifidelity control variates address this difficulty by coupling a small number of high-fidelity simulations with many evaluations of lower-complexity reduced models. In this work, we analyze the case in which the reduced model is replaced by a neural surrogate rather than evaluated through a classical numerical scheme. We show that the resulting estimator remains unbiased and that the change in the optimal variance induced by the neural approximation is controlled by the error between the exact low-fidelity observable and its neural approximation. This estimate is then combined with residual stability estimates for inhomogeneous Fokker--Planck and Bhatnagar--Gross--Krook surrogates. We also extend the analysis to several control variates and to an asymptotic-preserving (AP) hierarchy containing the limiting Euler observable. In the fluid limit, the optimal hierarchical variance converges to the variance associated with the limiting Euler control, while the contribution of the intermediate kinetic correction vanishes. Numerical tests based on micro--macro neural surrogates illustrate the predicted variance stability and the behavior of the AP hierarchy.

</details>

Result: The review provides an up-to-date and consolidated overview of RBF approximation methods, identifying key computational challenges and stability issues that must be addressed for efficient high-performance and parallel computation.

Conclusion: RBF-based approximation methods are increasingly important across application fields, but successful deployment in high-performance and parallel computing environments requires careful attention to stability issues and computational challenges.

Abstract: We present an up-to-date overview of approximation methods based on Radial Basis Function (RBF) techniques, which have recently attracted attention across various computational tools and application fields. This review study presents relevant results on RBF techniques, highlighting the associated computational challenges and stability issues that must be addressed when high-performance or parallel computation is required.

</details>


### [20] [An analytical-numerical approach to the Emden-Fowler equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01465)
*Mihai Halic*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the Emden-Fowler equation u''=-x^r u^p with initial conditions u(0)=1, u'(0)=0, studying how its solution depends on parameters r and p both analytically and numerically. Special focus is given to estimating the first zero x0 of the solution, and the findings are applied to two new problems: solving the equation backwards from x0, and transforming it into an overdetermined boundary value problem to determine solvability conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Emden-Fowler equation has important applications in astrophysics, and understanding the qualitative and quantitative behavior of its solutions as a function of parameters r and p is fundamental. Estimating the first zero x0 is particularly important, and the authors address two previously unexplored problems: backward solution from x0 and solvability of an overdetermined boundary value problem variant.

Method: The paper combines analytical techniques for parameter dependence with numerical simulations. It derives estimates for the first zero x0 as a function of r and p, and uses these results to investigate the backward problem starting from x0 and an overdetermined boundary value problem formulation.

Result: The paper provides qualitative and quantitative characterization of solution dependence on r and p, including estimates of the first zero x0. It also resolves the backward problem from x0 and determines when the overdetermined boundary value problem is solvable.

Conclusion: The study provides a comprehensive analysis of the Emden-Fowler equation's parameter dependence, offers new estimates for the first zero, and introduces and solves two novel problem formulations (backward integration and overdetermined BVP solvability) that extend the applicability of the classical equation.

Abstract: We investigate the Emden-Fowler equation $u''=-x^ru^p, u(0)=1, u'(0)=0$, with roots in astrophysics, and study the qualitative and quantitative dependence of its solution on the parameters; the analytical work is paralleled by numerical simulations. A special attention is given to estimating the first zero $x_0$ of $u$ in terms of $r, p$. The results are used to address two apparently new issues: first, we solve EF backwards starting from $x_0$; second, we transform it into an overdetermined boundary value problem and decide when is this solvable.

</details>


### [21] [On a boundary value problem related to radiative heat transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01472)
