### [100] [Mitigating ray effects in rarefied flow simulations using an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00514)
*Shuyang Zhang,Weidong Li,Ming Fang,Zhaoli Guo*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper extends an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities to deterministic methods, specifically incorporating it into the DUGKS framework (resulting in SDV-DUGKS) to mitigate ray effects in rarefied flow simulations. The method reduces memory usage by ~2/3 in 1D and 1-2 orders of magnitude in 2D flows compared to original DUGKS while achieving good accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ray effects are a known issue in rarefied flow simulations, and the existing ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities shows promise but needs to be extended to deterministic methods to improve memory efficiency and mitigate ray effects.

Method: The approach performs multiple independent subproblems, each using a small set of randomly sampled velocity points, then averages their solutions. This ensures the distribution function at any velocity can contribute to the final result, approximating highly refined velocity-space resolution without increasing memory requirements in any single subproblem. The strategy is incorporated into the DUGKS framework, yielding the SDV-DUGKS method.

Result: In the collisionless limit (Kn→∞): (1) For 1D compressible flows, SDV-DUGKS reduces memory usage by ~2/3 compared to original DUGKS while achieving good agreement; (2) For 2D compressible flows, SDV-DUGKS requires 1-2 orders of magnitude less memory than original DUGKS while achieving good agreement. Tested on Sod shock tube, 1D Riemann problem, 2D lid-driven cavity flow, and 2D Riemann problem.

Conclusion: The proposed SDV-DUGKS method serves as a reliable and effective tool for mitigating ray effects in rarefied flow simulations, offering substantial memory savings without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: In this work, a ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities is extended to deterministic methods for mitigating ray effects in rarefied flow simulations. The strategy involves performing multiple independent subproblems, each using a small set of randomly sampled velocity points, and then averaging their solutions to obtain the final result. The core idea is to ensure that the distribution function at any velocity can contribute to the final result, approximating highly refined velocity-space resolution without increasing the memory requirement in any single subproblem. We incorporate this strategy within the DUGKS framework, and the resulting method is denoted as SDV-DUGKS. To evaluate the performance of the proposed method, we compare SDV-DUGKS with the original DUGKS on several test cases: (a) the Sod shock tube problem, (b) the one-dimensional Riemann problem, (c) the two-dimensional lid-driven cavity flow, and (d) the two-dimensional Riemann problem. The results show that, in the collisionless limit $\mathrm{Kn} \to \infty$: (1) for one-dimensional compressible flows, SDV-DUGKS reduces memory usage by approximately 2/3 compared with that of the original DUGKS while achieving good agreement; (2) for two-dimensional compressible flows, SDV-DUGKS requires one to two orders of magnitude less memory than the original DUGKS while achieving good agreement. Based on these results, it can be concluded that the proposed method serves as a reliable and effective tool for mitigating ray effects in rarefied flow simulations.

</details>


### [101] [A memory-efficient deterministic method for multiscale gas flows using an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00521)
*Shuyang Zhang,Weidong Li,Ming Fang,Zhaoli Guo*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a memory-efficient deterministic method for multiscale gas flow simulations based on an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy using stochastic discrete velocities. The method replaces a large deterministic velocity set with multiple small random velocity sets, each solved independently with a deterministic multiscale scheme (DUGKS), achieving over 80% memory savings while maintaining comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic multiscale gas flow simulations suffer from the curse of dimensionality: the number of discrete velocities grows dramatically with velocity space dimension and Mach number, exhausting available memory and computational resources.

Method: The proposed method (SDV-DUGKS) uses an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy with stochastic discrete velocities. It replaces the conventional large deterministic velocity set with multiple small random velocity sets. Each random set defines a subproblem solved by a deterministic multiscale numerical scheme that computes macroscopic moments via Monte Carlo integration, and the final flow field is obtained by arithmetic averaging over all subproblems.

Result: Validation through three test cases: (a) 1D shock structure confirming feasibility, (b) 2D cavity flow, and (c) supersonic flow around a square cylinder. The 2D cases demonstrate over 80% memory savings compared to the deterministic counterpart while maintaining comparable accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed method markedly reduces memory demand for multiscale flow simulations and exhibits strong potential to alleviate the curse of dimensionality currently hindering deterministic multiscale numerical schemes from being applied to engineering problems.

Abstract: Deterministic multiscale gas flow simulations have long suffered from the curse of dimensionality: the number of discrete velocities increases dramatically with the velocity space dimension and the Mach number, exhausting available memory and computational resources. To address this issue, this paper proposes a memory-efficient deterministic method based on an ensemble-of-subproblems strategy using stochastic discrete velocities. This strategy transforms the originally computationally expensive problem into a series of independently and efficiently solvable subproblems. To be concrete, the proposed method replaces the conventional large deterministic velocity set with multiple small random velocity sets. Each random set defines a subproblem, which is solved by a deterministic multiscale numerical scheme that computes macroscopic moments via Monte Carlo integration. The final flow field is obtained by arithmetic averaging over all sub-problems. In this work, we employ the discrete unified gas kinetic scheme (DUGKS) for spatial discretization and term the resulting method SDV-DUGKS. To validate the proposed method, several numerical test cases are conducted, including (a) the one-dimensional shock structure, (b) the two-dimensional cavity flow, and (c) supersonic flow around a square cylinder. The results of the one-dimensional shock structure confirm the feasibility of the proposed method. The two-dimensional cases demonstrate that, compared to its deterministic counterpart, the proposed method saves more than 80% of memory usage while maintaining comparable accuracy. These results indicate that the proposed method markedly reduces memory demand for multiscale flow simulations and exhibits strong potential to alleviate the curse of dimensionality that currently hinders deterministic multiscale numerical schemes from being applied to engineering problems.

</details>


### [102] [Consistent and bound-preserving finite-volume WENO scheme for compressible two-/$N$-phase flows with Phase-Field mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00746)
*Ziyang Huang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A bound-preserving, consistent finite-volume WENO scheme is proposed for compressible multiphase flows with Phase-Field modeling, ensuring conservation, equilibrium, and bound preservation across N immiscible phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-order schemes for compressible multiphase flows must simultaneously satisfy consistency, conservation, equilibrium, and bound preservation. Prior WENO schemes often produce fictitious phases, local voids, or overfilling due to inconsistent reconstruction of masses and volume fractions, especially when N phases are involved.

Method: The WENO scheme uses weights based on relative smoothness between stencils and a coupled reconstruction of masses and volume fractions. Consistency of reduction and volume fraction summation to unity are enforced, and the scheme is integrated with a consistent and conservative Phase-Field method with adaptive mesh refinement, including a variant with a consistent limiter.

Result: The scheme is verified on various benchmark compressible two-phase and N-phase flows. It is further demonstrated on shock-induced cavity collapse and shock-vessel-bubble interaction problems, showing the necessity of bound preservation for high-order schemes and comparing different compressible multiphase flow models.

Conclusion: The proposed WENO scheme satisfies all required properties (consistency, conservation, equilibrium, bound preservation) for compressible multiphase flows with Phase-Field mechanism and demonstrates robust performance on complex shock-multiphase interaction problems.

Abstract: In the present study, we propose a consistent and bound-preserving finite-volume WENO scheme that satisfies the requirements of consistency, conservation, equilibrium, and bound preservation for compressible multiphase flows with the Phase-Field mechanism. The proposed WENO scheme is developed based on a new calculation of WENO weights determined by relative smoothness between stencils and on a coupled reconstruction of the masses and volume fractions. Consistency of reduction and volume fraction summation to unity are considered during the development so that fictitious phases, local voids, or overfilling are not produced numerically when there are $N$ ($N \geqslant 1$) different immiscible phases. The proposed WENO scheme is applied to the consistent and conservative Phase-Field method with adaptive mesh refinement enabled. Various benchmark compressible two- and $N$-phase flows are performed to verify the properties of the proposed WENO scheme as well as its variant with the consistent limiter. We finally demonstrate the capability of the proposed WENO scheme in shock-induced cavity collapse and shock-vessel-bubble interaction problems, with discussion of the necessity of bound preservation for high-order schemes and comparison of different compressible multiphase flow models.

</details>


<div id='physics.plasm-ph'></div>

# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [103] [On the ideal stability of the sheared-flow Z pinch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00291)
*Daniel W. Crews,Jackson C. Turner*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes sheared-flow Z-pinch stability in ideal MHD using analytic dispersion relations in the complex-frequency plane, showing that trans-Alfvénic flow stabilizes the kink instability but excites shear-driven instabilities (reflection modes and acoustic kinks) that dominate the spectrum, leaving ultimate stability to non-ideal physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies using growth rate calculations concluded that even trans-Alfvénic sheared flows fail to suppress the kink instability in the Z-pinch, but the authors suspect this conclusion is incomplete because it conflates the MHD kink with shear-driven instabilities that emerge at high Reynolds number.

Method: The authors compute the analytic dispersion function in the complex-frequency plane and regularize it into adiabatic and resonant parts. This allows them to describe how discrete modes emerge from and interact with the continuous spectrum, distinguishing the Doppler-shifted flow continuum effects on interchange vs. kink instabilities.

Result: The Doppler-shifted flow continuum overlaps the interchange instability branch at all wavenumbers (enabling stabilization with modest sub-Alfvénic flow), while the kink is shielded by a frequency gap requiring trans-Alfvénic flow for resonance. However, at the same stabilization threshold, shear-driven instabilities (reflection modes and acoustic kink) arise and dominate the ideal-MHD spectrum.

Conclusion: Ideal MHD correctly describes the stabilization mechanism but demonstrates that sheared-flow Z-pinch stability ultimately depends on non-ideal physics, including finite orbit width and dissipation.

Abstract: Sheared-flow Z-pinch stability has been studied within ideal MHD primarily through growth rate calculations, which find that even trans-Alfvénic sheared flows apparently fail to suppress the kink instability. Trans-Alfvénic sheared flow does stabilize the MHD kink but also excites shear-driven instabilities characteristic of high-Reynolds-number supersonic flow. This distinction is evident from the dispersion relations underlying the growth rates, computed here as the analytic dispersion function in the complex-frequency plane. Regularization splits this function into adiabatic and resonant parts describing how discrete modes emerge from and interact with the continuous spectrum. The Doppler-shifted flow continuum interacts with the interchange and kink instabilities in distinct ways. For interchange, the continuum overlaps the instability branch at all wavenumbers, so even sub-Alfvénic sheared flow stabilizes profiles modestly beyond the interchange threshold. The kink, by contrast, is shielded from the continuum by a frequency gap, and trans-Alfvénic flow is required to Doppler-shift the continuum into resonance with it, giving a geometric picture of the stabilization threshold. But shear-driven instabilities arise at this same threshold, including reflection modes and an acoustic kink. It is these shear-driven modes, not the original MHD instabilities, that dominate the ideal-MHD spectrum in trans-Alfvénic conditions. The ideal analysis thus describes the stabilization mechanism while showing that the stability of the sheared-flow Z pinch ultimately rests on non-ideal physics, including finite orbit width and dissipation.

</details>


### [104] [Ion stopping from bound and free electrons in plasmas: A channel-mixed RPA approach with average-atom orbitals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00797)
*Zachary A Johnson,Patrick J Adrian,Joshua A Leveillee,Charles E. Starrett*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A new channel-mixed random phase approximation (cmRPA) dielectric response function is developed to treat bound electron stopping in partially ionized plasmas, combining Lindhard free response with explicit average-atom bound state transitions. The model shows excellent agreement with experiments and TD-DFT for proton stopping near and above the Bragg peak, and is computationally efficient enough for systematic exploration of extreme conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bound electron contributions to ion stopping in partially ionized plasmas are typically treated coarsely despite contributing roughly equally to free electrons. Accurate treatment is needed for predictive modeling, but TD-DFT is computationally prohibitive for systematic studies.

Method: Develops a channel-mixed random phase approximation (cmRPA) dielectric response function that combines Lindhard free electron response with explicit average-atom bound state transitions, including bound-bound, bound-free, and free-free channels with their mixing.

Result: Excellent agreement with ambient condition experiments and TD-DFT simulations for proton stopping near and above the Bragg peak. Reveals non-trivial channel mixing effects, bound-bound stopping contributions at low velocity, and significant bound electron contributions to tungsten stopping at ICF peak compression. For Malko et al. (2022) warm dense matter experiments, bound stopping mismodeling is unlikely to explain the observed theory-experiment discrepancy within linear response theory.

Conclusion: The cmRPA approach provides accurate and computationally efficient treatment of bound electron stopping, enabling systematic exploration of extreme plasma conditions, while helping to rule out bound-state modeling as the source of the Malko experiment discrepancy.

Abstract: Ion stopping in partially ionized plasmas often receives roughly equal contributions from bound and free electrons, yet bound electron contributions are usually treated at a very coarse level, with the exception of costly time-dependent density functional theory (TD-DFT) simulations. At energies above the Bragg peak, where linear response methods are a good approximation, more accurate treatment is both feasible and critical for predictive modeling. We develop a channel-mixed random phase approximation (cmRPA) dielectric response function that combines Lindhard free response with explicit average-atom bound state transitions. Validating against ambient condition experiments and TD-DFT simulations, we demonstrate excellent agreement for proton stopping near and above the Bragg peak. The computational efficiency of our approach enables systematic exploration of extreme conditions across a wide range of densities and temperatures. We find non-trivial stopping effects from channel mixing between bound and free transitions via RPA, bound-bound stopping contributions at low velocity, and predict significant bound electron contributions to stopping in tungsten at peak compression conditions relevant to inertial confinement fusion experiments. Applying our model to the warm dense matter stopping experiment of Malko et al. (2022), we evaluate whether improved bound-state modeling could resolve the reported theory-experiment discrepancy, finding that within the constraints of linear response theory, bound stopping mismodeling is unlikely to be the source of the observed deficit.

</details>


### [105] [Investigation of high pressure capacitively coupled plasmas produced by electrons energized in DC sheath at powered electrode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01030)
*Anuravi Sharma,Ramesh Narayanan,Arti Rawat,Ashish Ganguli*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper experimentally investigates power absorption mechanisms in a 13.56 MHz capacitively coupled plasma (CCP) across a wide pressure range (5-600 mTorr) at ~10 W. Measurements of axial plasma parameter profiles and DC self-bias voltage reveal that ohmic heating is not the dominant power deposition mechanism. Instead, the DC sheath voltage drop accelerates electrons, with the RF field playing a novel role at higher pressures—transferring net power to electrons through its asymmetric negative-biased swing—while stochastic heating dominates only at the lowest pressure (~5 mTorr).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine the power absorption mechanism in capacitively coupled plasmas across a wide pressure range, as the understanding of how power is deposited into the plasma (ohmic vs. stochastic vs. sheath-driven heating) is fundamental to plasma physics and industrial applications.

Method: Axial profiles of plasma parameters were measured experimentally along with the DC self-bias voltage (V_DC) on the powered electrode, from which the DC sheath voltage drop (V_s) was determined. Electron ohmic power absorption profiles were computed, ionization mean free paths (λ_iz) were derived from probability arguments correlated with density peak locations, and independently calculated from electron acceleration/velocity gained in the DC sheath drop. Results were compared across pressures (5-600 mTorr).

Result: Power deposition is highest in low-density regions and lowest in high-density regions, ruling out Ohmic heating as the driving mechanism. Good agreement was found between λ_iz values calculated from density profiles and those from sheath-acceleration arguments at all pressures except the lowest (~5 mTorr). Electron sheath transit times are a small fraction of the RF period, meaning accelerated electrons experience RF as instantaneous spot values superimposed on the DC sheath drop. The negative self-bias makes the RF swing asymmetric and net power is transferred to electrons over many RF cycles—except at 5 mTorr where stochastic heating dominates.

Conclusion: At higher pressures, the RF field plays a novel, previously unreported role in powering the plasma: working in conjunction with the DC sheath voltage drop to transfer net energy to electrons. Stochastic heating dominates only at the lowest pressure (~5 mTorr), while Ohmic heating is not the primary power absorption mechanism across the investigated range.

Abstract: A 13.56 MHz, capacitively coupled plasma is investigated experimentally to determine the power absorption mechanism across a wide pressure range ({\approx} 5 - 600 mTorr) at {\approx} 10 W. Axial profiles of plasma parameters are measured along with V_{DC}, the DC self-bias voltage on the powered electrode (PE), from which the DC sheath voltage drop, V_{s} is determined. Axial profiles of electron ohmic power absorption reveal that power deposition is highest in low-density regions and lowest in high-density regions, indicating that plasma formation is not driven by Ohmic heating. Probability arguments were correlated with locations of the density peaks to determine the ionization mean free paths (λ_{iz}) at each pressure. Electron acceleration and average electron velocity acquired in the DC sheath voltage drop at PE were also calculated to determine λ_{iz} independently for comparing with those calculated from the density profiles. The agreement is good for all pressures, barring the lowest pressure ({\approx} 5 mTorr) for which there is significant deviation. The electron sheath transit times are a small fraction of the RF period, implying that the accelerated electrons experience the RF field as instantaneous "spot values" superimposed on V_{s}, the DC sheath drop. The negative self-bias on PE renders the RF swing asymmetric making it negative for most of the cycle. Since the RF accelerates electrons when it is negative and takes energy from them when it is positive, net power is transferred to the electrons during the course of steady-state measurements, averaged over many RF cycles. Except at {\approx} 5 mTorr where stochastic heating dominates, the RF field at higher pressures exhibits a novel role, not hitherto reported.

</details>


### [106] [Two-fluid boundary turbulence simulations in reversed field pinch plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01232)
*M. Giacomin,B. Momo,I. Predebon,N. Vianello,M. Zuin*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends the GBS boundary turbulence code to simulate turbulence in reversed field pinch (RFP) configurations, implementing non-large-aspect-ratio operators and 3D Poisson/Ampere solvers, then applies it to RFX-mod RFP plasmas. The simulations reproduce experimental measurements but reveal a fundamental limitation of fluid-based turbulence modeling in RFP edge regions due to the short parallel connection length, with gyrokinetic analysis identifying trapped electron modes as the dominant instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Turbulence in the plasma boundary governs confinement and wall interactions in fusion devices. While turbulence is typically studied in tokamak configurations with three-dimensional two-fluid flux-driven simulations, reversed field pinch (RFP) configurations—with their reversed magnetic field and chaotic magnetic topology—have not been adequately addressed. Existing codes rely on assumptions (large-aspect ratio, weak poloidal field) that break down for RFP geometry, motivating the need to extend turbulence simulation capabilities to RFP configurations.

Method: The GBS boundary turbulence code is extended for RFP configurations by: (1) modifying differential operators to avoid large-aspect-ratio and weak poloidal magnetic field approximations, (2) implementing three-dimensional Poisson and Ampere solvers to handle partially or fully disrupted magnetic flux surfaces (encompassing the reversal surface and arbitrary magnetic chaos), and (3) applying the modified code to simulate turbulence in the boundary of RFX-mod RFP plasmas. A linear gyrokinetic analysis is also performed to identify the dominant microinstabilities.

Result: Turbulent eddies across the reversal surface in RFPs show properties similar to those in tokamak boundary turbulence simulations, and good agreement is found with experimental measurements. However, the simulations reveal a significant limitation of fluid-based turbulence modeling for the RFP edge region, arising from the intrinsically short parallel connection length. Linear gyrokinetic analysis identifies trapped electron modes as the dominant microinstability in this region.

Conclusion: While the extended GBS code successfully simulates RFP boundary turbulence and matches experiments, the short parallel connection length in RFP edge regions fundamentally limits fluid-based turbulence modeling there. The gyrokinetic finding that trapped electron modes dominate suggests that kinetic treatment may be necessary for accurate RFP edge turbulence modeling.

Abstract: Turbulent transport in magnetic confinement fusion devices governs the overall plasma confinement properties and regulates the plasma-material interaction at the first wall. In the plasma boundary, turbulence is typically investigated through three-dimensional two-fluid flux-driven turbulence simulations. In this work, the GBS boundary turbulence code is extended to enable turbulence simulations in reversed field pinch configurations, encompassing the reversal surface and an arbitrary level of magnetic chaos. The differential operators implemented in the code are modified to avoid the approximations of large-aspect ratio and weak poloidal magnetic field. Three-dimensional Poisson and Ampere solvers are implemented to allow for turbulence simulations in conditions of partially or fully disrupted magnetic flux surfaces. This modified version of the GBS code is then applied to simulate turbulence in the boundary of RFX-mod reversed field pinch plasmas. Turbulent eddies across the reversal surface show properties similar to those typically found in tokamak boundary turbulence simulations. Despite the good agreement found with experimental measurements, these simulations reveal a significant limitation of the fluid-based turbulence modeling of the edge region in reversed field pinch plasmas, which arises from the intrinsically short parallel connection length. This conclusion is also supported by a linear gyrokinetic analysis that identifies trapped electron modes as the dominant microinstability in this region.

</details>


### [107] [Photon Orbital Angular Momentum Control by Electron Wavepackets in Nonlinear Compton Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01323)
*Zheng-Yang Zuo,Peng-Pei Xie,Xiang-Nan Shi,Yan-Fei Li*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a general analytical theory of nonlinear Compton scattering for arbitrarily shaped electron wave packets, showing that the transverse Fourier spectrum of the electron wave packet directly determines the OAM spectrum of emitted photons via a generalized angular-momentum selection rule, unifying various electron states into a single framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theories describe photon OAM in nonlinear Compton scattering primarily through angular-momentum transfer involving structured incident particles and laser fields, but the role of the electron wavepacket has remained unexplored. This gap motivates the need for a comprehensive theory incorporating arbitrarily shaped electron states.

Method: The authors develop a general analytical theory of nonlinear Compton scattering that accommodates arbitrarily shaped electron wave packets, deriving a generalized angular-momentum selection rule that connects the transverse Fourier spectrum of the electron wave packet to the OAM spectrum of emitted photons.

Result: The theory unifies Gaussian wave packets, vortex electrons, and arbitrarily shaped electron states within a single framework, revealing that the transverse Fourier structure of electron wave packets is a fundamental degree of freedom governing photon OAM, thus enabling deterministic engineering of photon OAM distributions.

Conclusion: The transverse Fourier structure of electron wave packets serves as a fundamental, previously unexplored degree of freedom for controlling photon orbital angular momentum in nonlinear Compton scattering, offering a path toward deterministic engineering of vortex gamma-ray sources.

Abstract: Photon orbital angular momentum (OAM) generated in nonlinear Compton scattering has attracted considerable interest as a route toward vortex $γ$-ray sources. Existing theories describe photon OAM primarily through angular-momentum transfer involving structured incident particles and laser fields, while the role of electron-wavepacket has remained unexplored. Here, we develop a general analytical theory of nonlinear Compton scattering for arbitrarily shaped electron wave packets and demonstrate that the Fourier spectrum of the transverse electron wave packet directly determines the OAM spectrum of the emitted photons through a generalized angular-momentum selection rule. Our theory unifies Gaussian wave packets, vortex electrons, and arbitrarily shaped electron states within a single framework, revealing the transverse Fourier structure of electron wave packets as a fundamental degree of freedom governing photon orbital angular momentum and enabling deterministic engineering of photon OAM distributions.

</details>


### [108] [Sub-sonic compressible magnetohydrodynamic turbulence I. Alfvénic and fast-magnetosonic injection, amplitude dependence, and compressibility effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01386)
*Eleonora Puzzoni,Silvio Sergio Cerri,Dimitri Laveder,Thierry Passot,Pierre-Louis Sulem*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper uses 3D ideal-MHD simulations (1024³, PLUTO code) to study how sub-sonic compressible MHD turbulence properties relevant to cosmic-ray transport depend on initial fluctuation nature/amplitude and plasma β, finding that turbulence characteristics (spectra, anisotropy, shock prevalence, magnetic curvature, mirror structures) are significantly sensitive to these conditions, with the -2.5 power-law emerging only for large amplitudes at high β.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the nature and amplitude of initial fluctuations, along with plasma compressibility β, affect the properties of sub-sonic compressible MHD turbulence, since these properties are key inputs for modeling cosmic-ray transport in the Galaxy.

Method: 3D simulations of decaying compressible ideal-MHD turbulence at 1024³ resolution using the PLUTO code, comparing Alfvénic versus fast-magnetosonic initial perturbations across different amplitudes (δB/B₀) and plasma β values.

Result: (1) Density fluctuation levels are insensitive to injection type once turbulence is fully developed. (2) Fast-mode injection shows early shock dissipation followed by turbulence-dominated decay. (3) Fast-mode contributions remain relevant only for pure fast-mode injection. (4) Large-amplitude turbulence yields nearly isotropic Kolmogorov (Alfvénic) or Iroshnikov-Kraichnan (fast) spectra; low-amplitude yields strongly anisotropic spectra with different behavior for fast modes (shock-dominated, isotropic k⁻²). (5) Magnetic curvature and mirror structures are highly sensitive to amplitude and β. (6) The -2.5 power-law only appears for large amplitudes at high β.

Conclusion: Features of sub-sonic compressible MHD turbulence relevant to CR transport are sensitive to large-scale initial conditions and plasma β, indicating that these parameters must be carefully accounted for when modeling cosmic-ray diffusion and field-line random walk in the Galactic environment.

Abstract: We investigate how sub-sonic compressible magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence properties that are relevant for cosmic-ray (CR) transport in the Galaxy are affected by the nature and amplitude of initial fluctuations, and by the plasma compressibility $β$. We perform 3D simulations of decaying compressible ideal-MHD turbulence at $1024^3$ resolution with the PLUTO code. The level of density fluctuations in fully developed turbulence is insensitive to whether this state is reached starting from Alfvénic or fast-magnetosonic perturbations. Fast-magnetosonic injection is characterized by an early phase of rapid shock dissipation, followed by a turbulence-dominated decay with a rate comparable to that of the Alfvénic case. The contribution of fast-magnetosonic fluctuations in fully developed turbulence remains relevant only when the initial injection consists exclusively of fast modes. Large-amplitude turbulence ($δB/B_0>1$) is characterized by a nearly isotropic Kolmogorov or Iroshnikov-Kraichnan spectrum for Alfvénic or fast-magnetosonic injection, respectively. At low amplitudes ($δB/B_0\ll1$), both initial Alfvénic and mixed-wave perturbations lead to strongly anisotropic turbulence with spectra $\propto k_\perp^{-5/3}$ and $\propto k_z^{-2}$ (becoming steeper at $β\gg1$), whereas fast-magnetosonic perturbations produce a turbulent state populated by shocks with a nearly isotropic $k^{-2}$ spectrum. Magnetic-field curvature and mirror structures are strongly sensitive to fluctuation amplitude and plasma $β$. The predicted -2.5 power-law scaling emerges only in the large-amplitude regime at high $β$. This work highlights that features of sub-sonic compressible MHD turbulence that may affect CR transport are sensitive to large-scale conditions and to the plasma $β$. Their effect on CR diffusion and field-line random walk is the object of Paper II.

</details>


### [109] [AGNI: A differentiable MHD stability solver & optimizer for magnetic confinement fusion devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01750)
*Rahul Gaur,Sanket Patil,Prateek Gupta,Djin Patch,Tony Qian*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: AGNI is a GPU-accelerated, automatically differentiable finite-n ideal MHD stability solver and optimizer that discretizes the ideal MHD energy principle pseudospectrally in real space using DESC equilibrium coefficients, enabling gradient-based optimization against finite toroidal mode number instabilities in tokamaks, stellarators, and mirrors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite toroidal mode number (n) instabilities degrade performance in both tokamaks and stellarators, yet existing differentiable stability optimization tools operate only in the infinite-n limit. There is a need for a finite-n stability solver that can be integrated into gradient-based optimization workflows.

Method: AGNI discretizes the ideal MHD energy principle pseudospectrally in real space using differentiation matrices and geometric coefficients from a DESC equilibrium, formulating a variational eigenvalue problem for plasma displacement. It is built on JAX, enabling reverse-mode gradients of the growth rate with respect to boundary-shape and profile parameters without re-solving the equilibrium. It also presents robust schemes to impose incompressibility compatible with gradient-based optimization.

Result: AGNI is benchmarked against NIMSTELL for a modified Landreman-Buller-Drevlak quasi-helically symmetric equilibrium, recovering the dominant m = n = 4 interchange mode with agreement in growth rate and eigenfunction structure. Gradients are verified against central finite differences. CPU and GPU costs are quantified, and the finite-precision limit on near-marginal eigenvalue resolution is established.

Conclusion: AGNI enables optimization of tokamaks, stellarators, and mirrors against ideal MHD instabilities in the finite-n regime, extending beyond the existing infinite-n-only differentiable stability optimization tools.

Abstract: The existence of an ideal MagnetoHydroDynamic (MHD) equilibrium does not guarantee its stability. Finite toroidal mode number (n) instabilities degrade performance in both tokamaks and stellarators and differentiable stability optimization tools to date have operated only in the infinite-n limit. We present AGNI (Analysis of Global Normal modes in Ideal MHD), a GPU-accelerated, automatically differentiable finite-n ideal MHD stability solver and optimizer. AGNI discretizes the ideal MHD energy principle pseudospectrally in real space using differentiation matrices and geometric coefficients from a DESC equilibrium, giving a variational eigenvalue problem for the plasma displacement, and efficiently finds the most unstable modes. Built on JAX, AGNI yields reverse-mode gradients of the growth rate with respect to boundary-shape and profile parameters without re-solving the equilibrium. We benchmark AGNI against the initial-value code NIMSTELL for a modified Landreman-Buller-Drevlak quasi-helically symmetric equilibrium, recovering the dominant m = n = 4 interchange mode with agreement in both growth rate and eigenfunction structure, and verify the automatic differentiation gradients against central finite differences. We quantify CPU and GPU cost for eigenvalue and gradient evaluation, establish the finite-precision limit on resolving near-marginal eigenvalues, and present a robust scheme to impose incompressibility, one of which is compatible with gradient-based optimization. AGNI will allow us to optimize tokamaks, stellarators, and mirrors against ideal MHD instabilities.

</details>


### [110] [Hybrid Kinetic-MHD Simulations of Drift-Orbit Effects on the Stability and Non-Linear Dynamics of Runaway Electron Beams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02121)
*Shi-Jie Liu,Hao-Wei Zhang,Hannes Bergstroem,Matthias Hoelzl,the JOREK Team*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates finite-orbit-width (FOW) effects of runaway electrons (REs) on tearing mode (TM) dynamics in tokamak disruptions using the hybrid fluid-kinetic JOREK code. Higher RE energy stabilizes MHD modes by preventing narrow current sheets on rational surfaces, counteracting the destabilizing effect seen at low energies, and modifies mode coupling via m=1 perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous studies treated REs with zero drift-orbit deviation (low-energy limit), showing destabilizing effects on tearing modes. This work addresses the gap by studying how finite orbit width—a kinetic effect—alters RE beam MHD stability and non-linear tearing mode dynamics.

Method: Use of a hybrid fluid-kinetic model in the 3D non-linear MHD code JOREK, treating REs kinetically with a full-f Monte Carlo approach in self-consistent interaction with MHD mode dynamics, examining both linear and non-linear TM dynamics across different RE energies.

Result: (1) Major-radial drift orbit displacement induces an m=1 perturbation to equilibrium current, adding mode coupling between (m,n) instabilities and (m±1,n) sidebands. (2) Increasing RE energy stabilizes MHD modes because REs cannot sustain narrow current sheets on rational flux surfaces due to orbit displacement. The stabilizing effect dominates over the additional mode coupling, reducing stochastic magnetic regions and lowering radial particle transport.

Conclusion: Finite-orbit-width effects substantially alter the MHD stability and non-linear dynamics of RE beams, representing an important physics ingredient that must be accounted for in RE stability predictions during disruptions.

Abstract: During tokamak disruptions, the Ohmic current may be replaced by a non-inductive runaway electron (RE) current, affecting resistive stability. Previous studies suggest that, in the linear phase, the presence of REs acts destabilizing for tearing modes (TM) compared to a scenario with Ohmic current. In the non-linear regime, this translates to larger saturation amplitudes. These results are based on the assumption of zero drift-orbit deviation from the magnetic flux surfaces corresponding to the low-energy limit. This work investigates the importance of this kinetic effect by studying the linear and non-linear TM dynamics in RE beams with different RE energies, providing a clear picture of finite-orbit-width (FOW) effects. We use a hybrid fluid-kinetic model in the 3D non-linear magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) code JOREK, treating REs kinetically with a full-f Monte Carlo approach in self-consistent interaction with the MHD mode dynamics. The study shows that the presence of REs modifies the characteristics of the instability in several ways. First, we find that the major-radial displacement of drift orbits from flux surfaces induces an $m=1$ perturbation to the equilibrium current, introducing additional mode coupling between $(m,n)$ instabilities and the $(m\pm1,n)$ sidebands. Second, we find that increasing RE energy has a stabilizing effect on the MHD modes because REs cannot support narrow current sheets on rational flux surfaces owing to the drift-orbit displacement. This counteracts the destabilizing effect that REs have on TMs in the low-energy limit. For the scenario investigated, the stabilizing effect dominates over the additional mode coupling, reducing the development of stochastic magnetic regions with increasing RE energy and thereby lowering radial particle transport. Overall, we find that FOW effects can substantially alter the MHD stability and non-linear dynamics of RE beams.

</details>


### [111] [Mass Spectrometry Studies of Hydrogen Ions Energy Distributions in an ECR- based Large Volume Plasma Source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02226)
*Bibekananda Naik,Ramesh Narayanan,Debaprasad Sahu,Mainak Bandyopadhyay,Ashish Ganguli*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents experiments using a compact ECR plasma source (CEPS) attached to a Large Volume Plasma Source (LVPS) to produce negative hydrogen ions (H-) at low pressures (1-3 mTorr) and moderate microwave power (400-600 W). Using mass spectrometry and Langmuir probe diagnostics, the authors measure H- ion energy distribution functions, positive ion spectra, and estimate plasma densities in the downstream region. They find a modest H- density of 3.9x10^8 cm^-3 downstream, but when accounting for transport losses, the estimated formation-zone H- density is 5.5x10^10 cm^-3, which is remarkably encouraging for volume-mode H- production given the large chamber and low power used.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to characterize and evaluate negative hydrogen ion (H-) production in volume mode using a large-volume plasma source driven by an ECR source, to determine whether favorable conditions for H- generation can be achieved at low pressures and moderate power in a large chamber useful for applications such as neutral beam injection for fusion or accelerator applications.

Method: The experimental method uses a compact ECR plasma source (CEPS) attached to a 1 m diameter by 1 m height Large Volume Plasma Source (LVPS), operated with CW microwaves (400-600 W, 2.45 GHz) at hydrogen pressures of 1-3 mTorr. Diagnostics include a Hiden Analytical HPR 60 molecular beam mass spectrometer (MBMS) to measure H- ion energy distribution functions (IEDFs) and positive ion spectra, plus a Langmuir probe (LP). Combined LP and MBMS data allow estimation of species densities downstream. Ion loss mechanisms (scattering and destruction) are accounted for to back-calculate densities in the H- formation zone (10-30 cm downstream of source exit).

Result: At 400 W and 1 mTorr, H- count rates of 3x10^5 counts/s were measured 80 cm downstream with a distinct high-energy tail up to 20 eV. Positive ion spectra showed H3+ dominating (60-70%), with H2+ at 30-35% and H+ at a few percent. At 500 W and 2 mTorr, combining LP and MBMS data gave densities of n(H+)=9.6x10^9 cm^-3, n(H2+)=1.7x10^10 cm^-3, and n(H3+)=4.3x10^10 cm^-3. The downstream H- density was 3.9x10^8 cm^-3, with an effective mean free path for H- loss of 12.4 cm. The estimated average H- density in the formation zone was 5.5x10^10 cm^-3.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the obtained H- density in the formation zone (5.5x10^10 cm^-3) is remarkably encouraging for volume-mode H- production, especially considering the large chamber volume and surface area, and the very moderate power used. This suggests the CEPS-LVPS configuration offers promising conditions for negative ion generation.

Abstract: Plasma is produced in a Large Volume Plasma Source (LVPS; dia. = 1 m, height = 1m) using CW microwaves (= 400 - 600 W, 2.45 GHz), in a compact ECR plasma source (CEPS) attached to LVPS, at hydrogen gas pressures = 1 - 3 mTorr. Plasma expands along the CEPS magnetic field into LVPS. A Hiden Analytical HPR 60 molecular beam mass spectrometer (MBMS) is used to measure the H^- ion energy distribution functions (IEDFs) in the downstream plasma. Previous plasma characterization studies in LVPS indicated favourable downstream plasma conditions for volume production of H^- ions. Measurements conducted with the MBMS probe aligned facing the plasma flow = 80 cm downstream, gave typical H^- count rates = 3 x 10^5 counts /s, at = 400 W, = 1 mTorr, along with a distinct high energy tail (<= 20 eV). These and other results are analyzed in detail. The positive ion spectrum showed the H_3^+ count to be consistently high in all cases (= 60-70 %); the counts for H_2^+ and H^+ were =30-35 % and a =few %. Combining the Langmuir probe (LP) and MBMS data it is possible to determine the approximate densities in front of the MBMS probe aperture. At = 500 W and = 2 mTorr, one finds: n_(H^+) = 9.6 x 10^9 cm^(-3), n_(H_2^+) = 1.7 x 10^10 cm^(-3) and n_(H_3^+) = 4.3 x 10^10 cm^(-3). The corresponding H^- density, = 80 cm downstream is n_(H^-) = 3.9 x 10^8 cm^(-3). Accounting for all H^- losses due to scattering and destruction, one finds the effective mean free path for H^- loss to be = 12.4 cm. Noting that H^- formation takes place about = 10 - 30 cm downstream of the source exit, the approximate average H^- density in the formation zone is determined as = 5.5 x 10^10 cm^(-3). This value is remarkably encouraging for H^- production in volume mode, considering the large chamber volume and area, as well as the very moderate power used for the experiments.

</details>


### [112] [Direct Optimization of Stellarator Omnigenity from the Second Adiabatic Invariant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02418)
*Hanlin Chen,Zhiyuan Lu,Guosheng Xu,Shuai Cao,Yang Han,Dehong Chen,Baonian Wan*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A differentiable optimization framework that directly optimizes the fundamental orbit action governing particle confinement in stellarators, yielding compact designs with excellent fast-ion confinement, finite-pressure stability, and coil compatibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stellarators suffer from enhanced particle losses due to their 3D geometry, but existing optimization methods rely on geometric proxies rather than the actual trapped-particle orbit condition, limiting their effectiveness in achieving good confinement.

Method: A differentiable framework that directly optimizes the fundamental orbit action governing particle confinement, integrating first-principles orbit optimization with engineering constraints in a single computational workflow.

Result: The approach yields compact stellarator designs with excellent fast-ion confinement, finite-pressure stability, and coil compatibility.

Conclusion: First-principles orbit optimization can be successfully integrated with engineering constraints in a unified computational workflow for fusion reactor design.

Abstract: Stellarators offer a steady-state, disruption-free path to fusion energy but suffer from enhanced particle losses due to their three-dimensional geometry. Existing optimization methods rely on geometric proxies rather than the actual trapped-particle orbit condition. We present a differentiable framework that directly optimizes the fundamental orbit action governing particle confinement. The approach yields compact stellarator designs with excellent fast-ion confinement, finite-pressure stability, and coil compatibility, demonstrating that first-principles orbit optimization can be integrated with engineering constraints in a single computational workflow for fusion reactor design.

</details>

### [113] [Machine-Learning-Accelerated Metallene Stabilization from High-Throughput Sandwich Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01779)
*Mohammad Bagheri,Pekka Koskinen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using universal machine-learning interatomic potentials, the authors constructed and analyzed 1620 metallene sandwich heterostructures (6 sandwich layers × 45 metals), identifying 1208 dynamically stable structures via phonon calculations. They found transition-metal dichalcogenides, especially MoSe2, are highly effective stabilizers, with buckled hexagonal and honeycomb lattices being most stable. Thermal stability was further confirmed via DFT-MD simulations, providing systematic design guidelines for metallene synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metallenes have appealing properties but are difficult to stabilize in monolayer form. A recent experiment demonstrated van der Waals squeezing can stabilize metallenes in MoS2 sandwiches, motivating systematic studies that are experimentally impractical and computationally prohibitive with first-principles methods.

Method: The authors used universal machine-learning interatomic potentials to build 1620 metallene sandwich heterostructures (6 sandwich layers × 45 metals), performed phonon calculations to assess dynamical stability, and validated selected structures with density-functional theory molecular dynamics (DFT-MD) simulations at room temperature.

Result: 1208 dynamically stable structures were identified. MoSe2 was found to be particularly effective at stabilizing metallenes, and buckled hexagonal and honeycomb crystal lattices showed the greatest stability. Selected heterostructures were confirmed thermally stable at room temperature via DFT-MD.

Conclusion: By revealing the physical and chemical factors governing metallene stabilization, the results provide systematic insights that can guide and accelerate metallene synthesis for future applications.

Abstract: Metallenes have appealing properties, but stabilizing them in a monolayer phase poses challenges for their synthesis. A recent experiment showed that the van der Waals squeezing method can stabilize certain metallenes in a MoS2 sandwich. This pioneering work motivates systematic studies, but such studies are experimentally impractical, while first-principles modeling remains prohibitive. Here, armed with universal machine-learning interatomic potentials, we constructed 1620 metallene sandwich heterostructures containing 6 different sandwich layers and 45 metals. We performed phonon calculations, which revealed 1208 dynamically stable structures. We found that transition-metal dichalcogenides, particularly MoSe2, are highly effective in stabilizing metallenes. Specifically, buckled hexagonal and honeycomb crystal lattices exhibit the greatest stability. We further evaluated the thermal stability of selected heterostructures with density-functional theory molecular dynamics simulations at room temperature. By uncovering the physical and chemical factors governing the stabilization of metallenes, our results provide systematic insights to guide and accelerate synthesis for future applications.

</details>

### [114] [Random Inverse Problems with Structural and Probabilistic Ambiguities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01439)
*Wolfgang Hoegele*

Main category: stat.ME

TL;DR: This paper studies random inverse problems with nonlinear model parameter uncertainties modeled via mixture densities, proposing Bayesian inversion algorithms for three observation scenarios and demonstrating their ability to resolve probabilistic ambiguities in 1D/2D quadratic models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random inverse problems with nonlinear parameter dependencies arise across various fields, and there is a need to understand and address structural ambiguities (from non-injective forward models) combined with probabilistic ambiguities (from mixture model uncertainties) in a unified framework.

Method: The authors formulate random inverse problems where forward model parameters are assigned mixture model densities with separate components, leading to complex forward, observation, and posterior models. They derive Bayesian inversion algorithms for three observation scenarios, yielding posterior densities for inputs given output samples or an observed output density.

Result: Applying the algorithms to 1D and 2D quadratic models, they numerically demonstrate which scenarios allow the proposed algorithm to resolve probabilistic ambiguities, showing that making residual structural ambiguities visible in the posterior and illustrating their interplay with probabilistic ambiguities is valuable, including cases with finite and infinite solution sets.

Conclusion: The mixture-model parameters in the forward model simultaneously encode both nonlinear structural ambiguity and uncertainty of the inverse problem. Making these ambiguities explicit in the posterior provides a relevant and useful perspective for solving random inverse problems.

Abstract: In this concise paper, we investigate a computational class of random inverse problems that incorporates model uncertainties through random variable parameters nonlinearly in the forward model as well as additive observational uncertainty. Random inverse problems with nonlinear parameter dependencies may arise in engineering, geophysics, image processing or uncertainty quantification. We introduce a new perspective on structural ambiguities due to the non-injectivity of the forward model together with probabilistic ambiguities by assigning mixture model densities with separate components to the parameters, which leads to a possibly complex forward model, observation model and posterior. As a result, the mixture-model parameters in the forward model can be interpreted as simultaneously describing aspects of both the nonlinear ambiguity and the uncertainty of the inverse problem. The underlying solution algorithm is presented based on Bayesian inversion for three observation scenarios leading to posterior densities for the input for given output samples or an observed output density. By applying the derived algorithms to 1D and 2D quadratic models, we numerically demonstrate in which scenarios the proposed algorithm can resolve probabilistic ambiguities in the solution of the random inverse problem. It is demonstrated that making the residual structural ambiguities visible in the posterior and showing the interplay with probabilistic ambiguities is a relevant perspective, including cases with a finite and an infinite number of solutions.

</details>


<div id='math.AG'></div>

# math.AG [[Back]](#toc)

### [115] [On the Datar-Mete-Song minimal slope conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01198)
*Xin Fu*

Main category: math.AG

TL;DR: This paper proves a conjecture of Datar-Mete-Song characterizing J-slope semi-stability via the minimal J-slope on compact Kähler manifolds, and introduces the J-null locus of a semi-stable pair, showing it is an analytic subset on compact Kähler surfaces and compact toric Kähler manifolds. It also establishes smoothness and Kähler-ness of Murakami's weak solution to the J-equation on the dense big torus in the toric invariant case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to prove a conjecture by Datar-Mete-Song that connects J-slope semi-stability (an algebraic/geometric stability notion) with the minimal J-slope (an analytic invariant), and to understand the structure of the J-null locus where such stability degenerates.

Method: The paper uses algebraic and analytic methods on compact Kähler manifolds, including big and nef birational test classes to characterize stability, and analyzes the J-null locus in special cases (Kähler surfaces and toric Kähler manifolds). For the toric invariant case, it examines Murakami's weak solution to the J-equation.

Result: (1) For semi-stable pairs of Kähler classes, every big and nef birational test class has slope at least the topological J-slope; unstable pairs admit a test class with strictly smaller slope. (2) The J-null locus is an analytic subset for compact Kähler surfaces and compact toric Kähler manifolds. (3) Murakami's weak solution to the J-equation is smooth and Kähler on the dense big torus in the toric invariant case.

Conclusion: The paper confirms the Datar-Mete-Song conjecture, establishing a precise correspondence between J-slope stability and minimal J-slope, and advances understanding of the J-null locus and the regularity of weak solutions to the J-equation in toric settings.

Abstract: We prove a conjecture of Datar-Mete-Song \cite{DMS} characterizing $J$-slope semi-stability by the minimal $J$-slope. More precisely, for a semi-stable pair of Kähler classes $(α,β)$ on a compact Kähler manifold $X$, every big and nef birational test class has slope at least the topological $J$-slope, whereas an unstable pair admits a test class with strictly smaller slope. We also introduce the $J$-null locus of a semi-stable pair and prove that it is an analytic subset of $X$ if $X$ is a compact Kähler surface or a compact toric Kähler manifold. In the toric invariant case, we show that Murakami's \cite{Murakami} weak solution to the $J$-equation is smooth and Kähler on the dense big torus $(\mathbb{C}^*)^n$ of $X$.

</details>
