### [116] [Sublinear Time Eigenvector Approximation via Column Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00840)
*Rajarshi Bhattacharjee,Cameron Musco,Dominic Rutkowski*

Main category: cs.DS

TL;DR: This paper presents sublinear-time sampling algorithms for approximating outlying eigenvectors of large symmetric matrices. The main algorithm uniformly samples only O(log n/ε⁴) columns and outputs an approximate eigenvector with error εn. For the top eigenvector, sampling reduces to O(log n/ε²) columns. With row/column norm-proportional sampling, improved error of ε||A||_F is achieved. The output eigenvectors are spanned by few sampled columns enabling poly(log n, 1/ε) per-entry computation, making them applicable to quantum-inspired algorithms. A truncated Nyström alternative achieves near-optimal sample complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing size of matrices in modern applications demands sublinear-time algorithms for fundamental tasks like eigenvector approximation. Prior sampling-based work tackled eigenvalue estimation, but extending these techniques to eigenvector estimation remained open. The paper is also motivated by the quantum-inspired algorithms framework, where classical algorithms must match quantum speedups—this requires sublinear sample and per-entry computation time for eigenvector approximation.

Method: The main algorithm uniformly samples O(log n/ε⁴) columns of a symmetric matrix A with bounded entries, and constructs an approximate eigenvector for any eigenvalue λ with |λ| ≥ εn. For the largest-magnitude eigenvalue, only O(log n/ε²) columns suffice. A variant using row/column sampling proportional to squared norms achieves improved error ε||A||_F. The output eigenvector is spanned by a small number of sampled columns, allowing poly(log n, 1/ε) time per entry. An alternative truncated Nyström-based approach achieves near-optimal sample complexity for general symmetric matrices and improved bounds for PSD matrices.

Result: The paper achieves: (1) uniform sampling with O(log n/ε⁴) columns for general outlying eigenvectors and O(log n/ε²) for the top eigenvector, both with εn error; (2) norm-proportional sampling with improved ε||A||_F error; (3) tightness of the top eigenvector bound up to logarithmic terms; (4) the first sublinear-time classical algorithms for eigenvector approximation in the quantum-inspired framework (extending Tang's STOC 2019 work); (5) near-optimal sample complexity via a truncated Nyström alternative.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates for the first time that recent random sampling approaches for eigenvalue estimation (from Bhattacharjee et al. and Swartworth & Woodruff) can be extended to eigenvector estimation. The results provide tight (up to log factors) bounds for top eigenvector approximation, enable quantum-inspired classical algorithms for eigenvector computation, and offer alternative trade-offs via truncated Nyström methods.

Abstract: We study sublinear time sampling methods for approximating the outlying eigenvectors of large matrices. Our main result is an algorithm that uniformly samples just $\tilde{O}(\log n/ε^4)$ columns of a symmetric matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ with entries bounded in magnitude by $1$, and, for any eigenvalue $λ$ of $A$ with $|λ| \ge εn$, outputs an approximate eigenvector $v$ satisfying $\|Av - λv\|_2 \le εn$. For approximating just the eigenvector of the largest magnitude eigenvalue, our algorithm samples only $\tilde{O}(\log n/ε^2)$ columns. Given the ability to sample rows and columns of $A$ proportional to their squared norms, we give a similar result with an improved error bound of $ε\|A\|_F$. For top eigenvector approximation, we show our bound is tight up to logarithmic terms.
  A key feature of our algorithms is that the output eigenvectors are spanned by a small number of $A$'s columns, and individual entries can be computed rapidly, in poly(log n, 1/epsilon) time per entry. This makes them applicable in the quantum-inspired algorithms framework of [Tang, STOC 2019], where we give the first sublinear time classical algorithms for eigenvector approximation with additive error $ε\|A\|_F$.
  Finally, we present an alternative approach, based on a truncated Nystrom method, that, while not allowing poly(log n, 1/epsilon) time entrywise computation of the approximate eigenvectors, achieves near optimal sample complexity for general symmetric matrices, and improved bounds for positive semidefinite matrices.
  Technically, our bounds build on recent work on approximating the outlying eigenvalues of symmetric matrices via random sampling in [Bhattacharjee et al. '22] and [Swartworth and Woodruff '25]. We demonstrate for the first time that these approaches extend to the problem of eigenvector estimation.

</details>


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [117] [Round-Trip Consistency: Bidirectional Diffusion Models Can Predict Their Own Rollout Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00675)
*Alexander Scheinker*

Main category: stat.ML

TL;DR: A single conditional latent diffusion model trained to step dynamical systems both forward and backward in time provides a self-supervised, measurement-free error signal via round-trip consistency (forward i steps then backward i steps must return to the start). The round-trip discrepancy serves as a proxy for unobservable rollout error, validated on MHD, turbulent radiative mixing, face videos, and turbulent Navier-Stokes benchmarks, achieving strong rank correlation with rollout error, OOD detection, and improved calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive models accumulate error over long rollouts, but at deployment there is no ground truth to measure this error against. The paper seeks a measurement-free way to estimate rollout error at test time without ensembles, held-out data, or governing equations.

Method: Train a single conditional latent diffusion model that can step a dynamical system forward or backward in time via a direction flag. Use round-trip consistency: rolling forward i steps then backward i steps should return to the start; the discrepancy C_i is a self-supervised test-time error proxy.

Result: On held-out MHD trajectories, C_i ranks rollout error with Spearman 0.91-0.98 at fixed depth (0.69 within trajectories); calibrator predicts magnitude within 1.14x (68%) and 1.29x (95%) confidence. Detects OOD Orszag-Tang vortex with AUROC 0.98 (1.0 by depth 10), cuts incurred error 15% at 80% coverage (3x depth-only baseline). Beats direction specialists in both directions, and on turbulent Navier-Stokes matches a ten-model ensemble within 1.3x accuracy at a tenth of training cost with best training-free calibration.

Conclusion: Round-trip consistency turns reversibility into a practical, training-free trust signal for generative models, providing error estimation, OOD detection, and calibration benefits at negative marginal training cost.

Abstract: Autoregressive models accumulate error over long rollouts, yet at deployment there is no ground truth to measure it against. We train a single conditional latent diffusion model that steps a dynamical system forward or backward in time via a direction flag, and show that this bidirectionality supplies a measurement-free test-time error signal: rolling forward $i$ steps and then backward $i$ steps must return the model to its start, so the round-trip discrepancy $\mathcal{C}_i$ is a self-supervised proxy for the unobservable rollout error: no ensembles, no held-out data, no governing equations, for one extra rollout. We validate on compressible magnetohydrodynamics (MHD), an astrophysical turbulent radiative mixing layer, and natural face videos (CelebV-HQ). On held-out MHD trajectories, $\mathcal{C}_i$ ranks rollout error (Spearman $0.91$-$0.98$ at fixed depth; $0.69 \pm 0.16$ within trajectories), and a simple calibrator fit on training rollouts predicts its magnitude to within $1.14\times$ ($68\%$) and $1.29\times$ ($95\%$) with near-nominal coverage - one nat beyond a depth-only predictor, transferring to all six decoded physical fields. The same signal flags the out-of-distribution Orszag-Tang vortex (AUROC $0.98$; $1.0$ by depth $10$) exactly where sampling-dispersion baselines invert, and it cuts incurred error by $15\%$ at $80\%$ coverage - three times the depth-only baseline. Bidirectional training comes at negative cost, beating direction specialists in both directions, and the backward direction doubles as a fast inverse solver. On LE-PDE-UQ's turbulent Navier-Stokes benchmark, a single bidirectional model reaches accuracy within $1.3\times$ of their ten-model ensemble at a tenth of the training cost, with the best training-free pixel-level calibration. Round-trip consistency turns reversibility into a practical trust signal for generative models.

</details>


<div id='math.OC'></div>

# math.OC [[Back]](#toc)

### [118] [Truncated Differentiation for Inverse Potential MFGs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00217)
*Siting Liu,Yat Tin Chow,Samy Wu Fung*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper introduces JFB-r, a Jacobian-free backpropagation method for inverse potential mean-field games (MFGs), which truncates differentiation to the final r iterations of a preconditioned PDHG solver. The authors prove that the PDHG map is a metric resolvent of the maximal monotone KKT operator, showing JFB-r exactly differentiates a finite-trajectory surrogate and converges to the implicit gradient as r increases. Experiments show JFB-r achieves comparable recovery accuracy to full unrolling with reduced memory and runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse mean-field games involve inferring unknown spatial mobility/cost maps from observed population densities, which is computationally challenging due to the need to differentiate through a forward MFG solve. Full unrolling of iterative solvers for backpropagation is memory-intensive and slow, so a more efficient differentiation strategy is needed.

Method: The forward MFG is solved using a preconditioned primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method. For differentiation, JFB-r records only the final r iterations from a detached warm start while retaining the full forward solve. The authors establish that the exact-proximal dual-extrapolated PDHG map acts as a metric resolvent of the maximal monotone KKT operator, providing a theoretical basis for the truncated differentiation.

Result: Numerical experiments across several inverse-MFG settings demonstrate that JFB-r with moderate tracked depths achieves recovery accuracy comparable to full unrolling while significantly reducing memory usage and runtime.

Conclusion: The resolvent-based analysis provides theoretical justification for truncated differentiation in PDHG-based inverse MFGs, showing convergence to implicit gradients under a locally fixed active set at an exact equilibrium detach point, making JFB-r a practical and efficient alternative to full unrolling.

Abstract: We study inverse potential mean-field games (MFGs), in which an unknown spatial inverse-cost (mobility) map is inferred from observed population densities. We solve the forward MFG with a preconditioned primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method and develop Jacobian-free backpropagation (JFB-$r$), which records only the final $r$ iterations from a detached warm start while retaining the full forward solve. To analyze this truncated differentiation method, we show that the exact-proximal dual-extrapolated PDHG map is a metric resolvent of the maximal monotone KKT operator. This resolvent view shows that JFB-$r$ exactly differentiates a finite-trajectory surrogate and, under a locally fixed active set at an exact equilibrium detach point, converges to the implicit gradient as the tracked depth increases. Across several inverse-MFG settings, numerical experiments show that JFB-r at moderate tracked depths can achieve recovery accuracy comparable to full unrolling while reducing memory and runtime.

</details>


### [119] [Revisiting Decomposition-Invariant Conditional Gradient Methods for Polytopes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01243)
*Dan Garber*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper revisits Decomposition-Invariant Conditional Gradient methods for minimizing convex β-smooth functions over polytopes under quadratic growth conditions, proposing parameter-free methods with linear convergence rates that scale with the dimension of the optimal face rather than the ambient dimension, significantly improving upon prior conditional gradient approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard away-step-based conditional gradient methods for polytopes have convergence rates that scale with the ambient dimension n, which can be wasteful when the optimal face has much lower dimension. The paper aims to design methods whose complexity depends on the intrinsic dimension of the optimal face (d*) rather than n, and to be parameter-free (not requiring knowledge of growth constants).

Method: For 2-level polytopes, the authors design a simple parameter-free dyadic step-size rule achieving linear convergence with rate scaling in the optimal face dimension. For general polytopes, under α_F-facial quadratic growth, they introduce a new method with iteration complexity n + βD²/(αr*²) + (d*+1)βD²/α_F · log(1/ε), using only standard line-search computations and no hyperparameter tuning.

Result: The proposed methods achieve convergence rates depending on the optimal face dimension d* instead of ambient dimension n. The second result improves upon previous conditional gradient methods (which scale as (βD²n/α)·log(1/ε)) in the meaningful regime where max{α/α_F · (d*+1), 1/r*²} ≪ n.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that dimension-free (relative to ambient space) linear convergence is attainable for conditional gradient methods on polytopes via parameter-free dyadic step-size rules and a facial quadratic growth condition, offering significant improvements over existing methods in structured regimes.

Abstract: We revisit Decomposition-Invariant Conditional Gradient methods, originally introduced by Garber and Meshi in 2016, for minimizing a convex and $β$-smooth function over a polytope in $\reals^n$, under an $α$-quadratic growth condition. For 2-level polytopes we design a simple and parameter-free dyadic step-size rule that yields a linear convergence rate which scales with the dimension of the optimal face and not with the ambient dimension as in standard away-step-based conditional gradient methods for polytopes. For general polytopes, under a slightly stronger condition of $α_{\mathrm{F}}$-\textit{facial quadratic growth}, we introduce a method whose number of iterations to reach an $ε$-approximate solution is of the order $n+\frac{βD^2}{αr^{*2}} + \frac{(d^*+1)βD^2}{α_{\mathrm{F}}}\log(1/ε)$, where $d^*$ is the dimension of the optimal face, $r^*$ is a separation parameter between the optimal set and faces that do not contain an optimal solution, and $D$ is the diameter of the polytope. This method is also parameter-free and only relies on standard line-search computations. The second result improves upon previous conditional gradient methods, whose number of iterations to $ε$-approximation scales with $\frac{βD^2n}α\log(1/ε)$, in a meaningful regime $\max\{\fracα{α_{\rm F}}(d^*+1), \frac{1}{r^{*2}}\} \ll n$

</details>


### [120] [A Geometric Inverse Source Problem for Stochastic Parabolic Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01351)
*Yunzhang Li,Qi Lü,Meizhi Qian,Yu Wang*

Main category: math.OC

TL;DR: This paper solves the geometric inverse problem of simultaneously recovering two unknown deterministic source supports in a stochastic parabolic equation (one in the drift term, one in the diffusion term), proving unique identifiability from partial boundary flux measurements, establishing existence of minimizers for a perimeter-regularized functional, and deriving a Hadamard-type shape derivative formula with a two-channel stochastic structure that enables a shape-gradient reconstruction method validated numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses a challenging geometric inverse problem in SPDEs: simultaneously recovering two distinct source supports (one in the drift, one in the diffusion term) from boundary measurements. No prior Hadamard-type shape derivative formula existed for this simultaneous recovery setting in SPDEs, motivating the development of new theoretical and computational tools.

Method: The authors establish uniqueness of both source supports from partial boundary flux measurements, prove existence of minimizers for a perimeter-regularized objective functional, and derive a Hadamard-type boundary representation of the shape derivative for smooth interfaces. The derivative is decomposed into a two-channel structure: the adjoint state governs drift-source sensitivity while the martingale component governs diffusion-source sensitivity. This formula is used to develop a shape-gradient reconstruction algorithm.

Result: The paper proves uniqueness of simultaneous recovery of both source supports, existence of perimeter-regularized minimizers, and provides the first Hadamard-type shape derivative formula for simultaneous drift/diffusion source support recovery in an SPDE setting. Numerical experiments confirm the method's effectiveness and its ability to distinguish between the two source channels under both full and partial boundary observations.

Conclusion: The simultaneous recovery of drift and diffusion source supports in stochastic parabolic equations is feasible from partial boundary measurements, with a rigorously grounded shape-gradient method enabled by the novel two-channel stochastic structure of the shape derivative.

Abstract: This paper addresses the geometric inverse problem of simultaneously recovering two unknown deterministic source supports in a stochastic parabolic equation, where one source appears in the drift term and the other in the diffusion term. We establish that partial boundary flux measurements alone uniquely determine both supports. Moreover, we prove the existence of minimizers for a perimeter-regularized objective functional. For smooth interfaces, we derive a Hadamard-type boundary representation of the shape derivative. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first such formula for the simultaneous recovery of a drift-source support and a diffusion-source support in an SPDE setting. The derivative exhibits a genuinely stochastic two-channel structure: the adjoint state governs the sensitivity of the drift-source interface, while the martingale component controls the sensitivity of the diffusion-source interface. Based on this formula, we develop a shape-gradient reconstruction method. Numerical experiments demonstrate its effectiveness and its capacity to distinguish between the two source channels under both full and partial boundary observations.

</details>


<div id='cs.CV'></div>

# cs.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [121] [MBO Scheme for Local Chan--Vese Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00893)
*Kevin Bui,Adina Ciomaga*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper develops a Merriman-Bence-Osher (MBO) scheme-based algorithm to efficiently solve the Local Chan-Vese (LCV) image segmentation model, covering two-phase and multiphase segmentation with an extension to color images, and demonstrates effectiveness on grayscale, color, medical, and microscopy images.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The LCV model improves upon the classical CV model by incorporating local statistical information, but its original finite difference solution is computationally expensive. Since an MBO-based algorithm was shown to be more efficient for the CV model, the authors aim to derive a similar MBO-based algorithm for the LCV model to achieve faster and more efficient segmentation.

Method: The authors derive an MBO-based algorithm to solve the LCV model, which replaces the traditional finite difference scheme with the more efficient MBO threshold dynamics approach. The algorithm is formulated for both two-phase and multiphase segmentation, and an extension to color images is also discussed.

Result: The proposed MBO-based LCV algorithm is applied to a variety of grayscale and color images, including medical and microscopy images, demonstrating its effectiveness in segmentation tasks.

Conclusion: The MBO-based approach provides an efficient alternative to the finite difference scheme for solving the LCV model, extending its applicability to two-phase, multiphase, and color image segmentation with demonstrated effectiveness across diverse image types.

Abstract: Robust to intensity inhomogeneity, the local Chan--Vese (LCV) model extends the classical Chan--Vese (CV) image segmentation method by incorporating local statistical information around each pixel. Originally, the LCV model was solved using a finite difference scheme, following the approach used for the CV model. As an alternative to the finite difference scheme, a more efficient algorithm based on the Merriman-Bence-Osher (MBO) scheme was later developed for the CV model. In this paper, we derive a similar MBO-based algorithm to solve the LCV model and propose an efficient implementation. The algorithm is developed for both two-phase and multiphase segmentation, and an extension to color images is also discussed. To demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, we apply it to a variety of grayscale and color images, including medical and microscopy images.

</details>


### [122] [The Push-Forward Transform for Continuous and Robust Comparison of Dynamic Shapes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02306)
*Roua Rouatbi,Juan-Esteban Suarez Cardona,Ivo F. Sbalzarini*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Push-Forward Transform (PF-T) applied to Signed Distance Functions (SDFs) as a mathematical framework for shape comparison that is invariant to transformations like translation, rotation, reflection, re-parametrization, and uniform scaling, while remaining sensitive to intrinsic geometric variation. The method captures both boundary and interior geometry, works in 2D and 3D, extends to time-evolving shapes, and supports joint analysis of shape with additional scalar fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantitative shape comparison is fundamental in image analysis, but existing approaches rely on sensitive parameterizations, landmark correspondence, or difficult-to-interpret learned representations. There is a need for representations that are invariant to shape-preserving transformations while remaining sensitive to intrinsic geometric variation.

Method: The Push-Forward Transform is applied to Signed Distance Functions, mapping functions from the shape domain to a common reference domain. This yields a continuous representation capturing both boundary and interior geometry, enabling an interpretable morphometric for shape similarity.

Result: The framework captures shape features such as skeletal topology and rotational symmetries, applies consistently to 2D and 3D shapes, extends to time-evolving geometries, and supports joint analysis of shapes with additional scalar fields. Benchmarks are demonstrated on 2D, 3D, and temporal datasets.

Conclusion: The Push-Forward Transform on SDFs provides a robust, interpretable, and reproducible framework for shape comparison that generalizes across dimensions and temporal data while preserving intrinsic geometric information.

Abstract: We introduce a mathematical framework for shape comparison based on mapping functions from the shape domain to a common reference domain. This Push-Forward Transform enables invariant and robust comparison of shapes, preserving intrinsic geometric information. Quantitatively comparing shapes and their temporal evolution is a fundamental challenge in image analysis. Meaningful shape comparison requires representations that are invariant to transformations that do not alter shape itself, such as translation, rotation, reflection, re-parametrization, and uniform scaling, while remaining sensitive to intrinsic geometric variation. Existing approaches often rely on sensitive parameterizations, landmark correspondence, or learned representations that are difficult to interpret and reproduce. We show that the Push-Forward Transform (PF-T) applied to Signed Distance Functions (SDFs) yields a continuous representation that captures both boundary and interior geometry. We derive an interpretable morphometric that quantifies shape similarity and reveals features such as skeletal topology and rotational symmetries. The push-forward transform applies consistently to two- and three-dimensional shapes, extends to time-evolving geometries, and supports the joint analysis of shape and additional scalar fields defined over shapes, such as intensity or molecular signals. We present the mathematical formulation, describe an efficient algorithm, and benchmark the approach on 2D, 3D, and temporal data sets.

</details>


<div id='cs.MS'></div>

# cs.MS [[Back]](#toc)

### [123] [Data-Driven Model Order Reduction with pyMOR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00082)
*Hendrik Kleikamp,Petar Mlinarić,Stephan Rave,Felix Schindler*

Main category: cs.MS

TL;DR: This paper discusses the recent addition of data-driven model order reduction (MOR) methods to pyMOR, a free and open-source Python library for MOR algorithms. It shows practical examples, compares data-driven methods with classical model-based approaches, and demonstrates how pyMOR serves as a unified framework combining both paradigms into flexible hierarchical model reduction pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical model-based MOR methods require tight integration with full-order model solver codes, which is not always feasible. Data-driven MOR methods that only require simulation or measurement data offer an attractive alternative, motivating their integration into pyMOR.

Method: The paper introduces data-driven MOR algorithms recently added to pyMOR, implemented alongside existing model-based methods through abstract VectorArray, Operator, and Model interfaces. It demonstrates their application through practical examples and benchmarks them against classical methods.

Result: pyMOR is shown to effectively serve as a unified framework combining both model-based and data-driven MOR methods, enabling the construction of flexible and efficient hierarchical model reduction pipelines with competitive performance between the two approaches.

Conclusion: The unified integration of data-driven and model-based methods in pyMOR provides a versatile platform for model order reduction, allowing users to select and combine approaches based on the availability and feasibility of full-order model integration.

Abstract: pyMOR is a free and open-source software library of model order reduction algorithms for the Python programming language. Designed with classical model-based reduction methods for large-scale parametric partial differential equation problems in mind, algorithms in pyMOR are implemented in terms of operations on abstract VectorArray, Operator and Model interfaces, allowing for a seamless integration with external solver codes implementing the full-order model. For cases where a tight integration with the full-order model code is not feasible, data-driven model order reduction algorithms, which only require simulation or measurement data of the full-order model, are an attractive alternative. In this work we discuss the data-driven methods that have been recently added to pyMOR, show practical examples of their application using pyMOR and compare their performance with classical model-based methods. We show that pyMOR serves as a unified framework for combining model-based and data-driven methods, enabling the construction of flexible and efficient hierarchical model reduction pipelines.

</details>


<div id='math.PR'></div>

# math.PR [[Back]](#toc)

### [124] [On the role of positivity preservation for high order approximations of the Dean--Kawasaki equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00838)
*Ana Damnjanović,Ana Djurdjevac,Nicolas Perkowski*

Main category: math.PR

TL;DR: This paper studies a spectral regularization of the Dean-Kawasaki equation and analyzes how the failure of positivity preservation impacts its weak approximation of the empirical measure of independent Brownian particles. The authors prove uniform-in-time weak error bounds via Laplace transform with superpolynomial convergence for smooth test functions when initial densities are bounded away from zero, but show weaker bounds and rule out superpolynomial convergence when initial densities vanish, supported by numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Dean-Kawasaki equation is known to lack well-posedness and positivity preservation under standard regularizations, yet such regularized versions are commonly used numerically. Understanding the quantitative impact of positivity loss on the accuracy of weak approximations to the empirical measure is crucial for assessing the reliability of these numerical schemes.

Method: The authors employ a spectral (Fourier-based) regularization of the Dean-Kawasaki equation. They analyze the weak approximation error through the Laplace transform of the regularized solution versus the true empirical measure, deriving uniform-in-time error bounds. They complement this with a one-dimensional lower bound example and numerical experiments.

Result: For initial densities bounded away from zero, a uniform-in-time weak error bound with superpolynomial convergence rate for smooth test functions is proven. When the initial density can vanish, the negative part of the regularized solution produces weaker upper bounds, and a 1D example delivers a lower error bound that rules out superpolynomial convergence. Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The positivity-preservation failure of spectral regularizations of the Dean-Kawasaki equation introduces errors whose severity depends critically on whether initial densities are bounded away from zero. The results provide precise quantitative characterizations of when superpolynomial convergence holds and when it cannot, guiding the practical use of such regularizations.

Abstract: We study a spectral regularization of the Dean--Kawasaki equation and quantify how the failure of positivity preservation affects its weak approximation of the empirical measure of independent Brownian particles. For initial densities bounded away from zero, we prove a uniform-in-time weak error measured through the Laplace transform and prove a superpolynomial convergence rate for smooth test functions. When the initial density is allowed to vanish, the negative part of the regularized solution leads to weaker upper bounds, and a one-dimensional example gives a lower error bound ruling out superpolynomial convergence. We also present numerical experiments that confirm the theoretical results and illustrate main observations.

</details>


<div id='physics.ao-ph'></div>

# physics.ao-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [125] [FESOM2-JAX v1.0: a differentiable shadow of the ocean-sea-ice model FESOM2, cast onto GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01546)
*Nikolay V. Koldunov,Sergey Danilov,Suvarchal Cheedela,Dmitry Sidorenko,Sebastian Beyer,Patrick Scholz,Ivan Kuznetsov,Jan Streffing,Aleksei Koldunov,Dmitrii Pantiukhin,Svetlana N. Loza,Thomas Jung*

Main category: physics.ao-ph

TL;DR: FESOM2-JAX is a Python re-implementation of the FESOM2 ocean-sea-ice model in JAX, retaining the original's unstructured-mesh finite-volume formulation. It runs from laptop to 256 GPUs, is end-to-end differentiable, and matches the Fortran version's results to high accuracy over a multi-decade hindcast. It is the first CMIP-class global ocean-sea-ice model natively written in a differentiable framework on an unstructured mesh.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To lower the barrier to experimentation in ocean modeling—enabling new numerics, parameterizations, gradient-based calibration, and hybrid physics-machine-learning components—by creating a code shadow of the Fortran FESOM2 in the Python/JAX ecosystem while keeping it close enough to the original for bidirectional transfer of developments.

Method: The authors re-implement FESOM2 in JAX using large language models for code translation, verifying each kernel against the Fortran original. The model retains the unstructured-mesh, cell-vertex finite-volume formulation. It supports scaling from a laptop CPU to 256 GPUs and provides reverse-mode differentiation through the full time loop.

Result: In a 1958-2019 hindcast at 1° resolution, the JAX and Fortran versions differ two orders of magnitude less than either differs from observations, agreeing for six decades across temperature, salinity, heat content, and sea ice. The 1° config fits on a single GPU; four GH200 superchips integrate ~113 simulated years per wall-clock day; meshes up to 7.4M surface vertices scale to 128 GPUs. Communication, not arithmetic, is the bottleneck. Reverse-mode gradients were verified against finite differences.

Conclusion: FESOM2-JAX demonstrates that a CMIP-class ocean-sea-ice model can be natively implemented in a differentiable framework on unstructured meshes, providing both high-performance scalability and end-to-end automatic differentiation for the ocean modeling community.

Abstract: We present FESOM2-JAX, a Python re-implementation of the Finite-volumE Sea ice-Ocean Model (FESOM2) in JAX. The model retains the unstructured-mesh, cell-vertex finite-volume formulation of the original, runs unchanged from a laptop CPU to 256 GPUs, and is end-to-end differentiable. FESOM2-JAX is a code shadow of the Fortran model: a projection onto the Python ecosystem, translated with large language models and verified kernel by kernel against the original. It is built to lower the barrier to experimentation, from new numerics and parameterizations to gradient-based calibration and hybrid physics-machine-learning components, while remaining close enough to the original so that what is developed in the shadow can be transferred back. In a 1958-2019 hindcast at 1$^{\circ}$ equivalent resolution with identical physics and forcing, the mean states of the JAX and Fortran versions differ from each other by two orders of magnitude less than either differs from observations, and the two runs agree for six decades in global temperature, salinity, heat content, and sea ice. The complete 1$^{\circ}$ configuration fits on a single GPU, a node of four GH200 superchips integrates $\sim$113 simulated years per wall-clock day, and meshes of up to 7.4 million surface vertices ($\sim$5 km) scale to 128 GPUs. What limits the model is communication rather than arithmetic. What the shadow adds to the original is the gradient: a single reverse-mode pass through the full time loop returns the sensitivity of a model diagnostic to a parameter at every mesh vertex, verified against finite differences. To our knowledge, FESOM2-JAX is the first global ocean-sea-ice model of CMIP-class complexity written natively in a differentiable framework, and the first on an unstructured mesh.

</details>

# math.FA [[Back]](#toc)

### [126] [A Paley-Wiener theorem for multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01139)
*Chikh Bouzar*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: This paper establishes a genuine Paley-Wiener theorem for multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable function spaces and introduces novel properties of these spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The goal is to extend the classical Paley-Wiener theorem—which characterizes functions with compact support via the growth of their Fourier transforms—to the setting of multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable functions, a framework where different directions may have different regularity scales.

Method: The authors prove a real Paley-Wiener theorem (in the real-domain sense, relating support properties to growth estimates of derivatives or Fourier transforms) tailored to the multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable function spaces, and additionally derive new structural properties of these spaces.

Result: A genuine (real) Paley-Wiener theorem is obtained for multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable functions, together with new properties characterizing this function space.

Conclusion: The paper contributes a significant theoretical advance by establishing Paley-Wiener-type characterizations and new structural results for multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable spaces, which have applications in analysis and partial differential equations.

Abstract: The present work presents a real Paley-Wiener theorem within the space of multi-anisotropic ultradifferentiable functions. We also show new properties of this space.

</details>


### [127] [Metric gradient flows and variational principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01262)
*Alberto Domínguez Corella*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that Ekeland-type variational principles can be obtained from steepest descent curves in metric spaces, and connects the existence of the Calabi flow to the validity of the Ekeland variational principle for the K-energy on Kähler potentials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a bridge between variational principles in metric spaces and geometric flows, specifically linking the Calabi flow existence to a variational framework for the K-energy.

Method: Derive Ekeland-type variational principles from steepest descent curves in metric spaces, then apply this framework to relate the Calabi flow existence to the Ekeland variational principle for the K-energy on the space of Kähler potentials.

Result: Ekeland-type variational principles can be derived from steepest descent curves in metric spaces, and this provides a connection between the existence of the Calabi flow and the validity of the Ekeland variational principle for the K-energy.

Conclusion: The existence of the Calabi flow is linked to the validity of the Ekeland variational principle for the K-energy on Kähler potentials, establishing a novel variational-geometric connection.

Abstract: We show that Ekeland-type variational principles can be derived from steepest descent curves in metric spaces. From this, we relate the existence of the Calabi flow to the validity of the Ekeland variational principle for the $K$-energy on the space of Kähler potentials.

</details>


<div id='math.SP'></div>

# math.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [128] [Capacity estimates and improved lower bounds for the inner radius of nodal domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01345)
*Philippe Charron*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: The paper establishes lower bounds on the size of geodesic balls contained within nodal domains of Laplace eigenfunctions on closed smooth manifolds of dimension d≥3, showing the inner radius is at least c(g)λ^{-1/2}(log log λ)^{-1/2} for d=3 and c(g)λ^{-1/2}(log λ)^{-(d-3)/2} for d>3, while also constructing examples on the torus showing these bounds cannot be improved to order λ^{-1/2}.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the geometry and size of nodal domains of Laplace eigenfunctions is a fundamental problem in spectral geometry. Determining how large a ball can be guaranteed inside any nodal domain provides crucial insight into the geometric structure of eigenfunctions and their zero sets.

Method: The authors prove analytic lower bounds for the inner radius of nodal domains in dimensions d≥3, centered at points where the eigenfunction attains maximum absolute value within the domain. They complement this with explicit constructions on the d-dimensional torus exhibiting nodal domains with inner radius of order o(λ^{-1/2}).

Result: For any closed smooth manifold (M,g) of dimension d, every nodal domain contains a geodesic ball of radius at least c(g)λ^{-1/2}(log log λ)^{-1/2} when d=3, and c(g)λ^{-1/2}(log λ)^{-(d-3)/2} when d>3. Additionally, for d≥3, nodal domains on the torus T^d exist with inner radius of order o(λ^{-1/2}), indicating near-optimality of the bounds.

Conclusion: The results provide quantitative lower bounds for the inner radius of nodal domains in dimensions d≥3, improving our understanding of the sharp scaling behavior. The torus constructions demonstrate that the bounds are essentially sharp in the sense that inner radius cannot always be of order λ^{-1/2}.

Abstract: We show that for every closed, smooth manifold $(M,g)$ of dimension $d$, there exists $c(g)$ such that any nodal domain $Ω_λ$ of a Laplace eigenfunction with eigenvalue $λ$ contains a geodesic ball of radius at least $c(g) λ^{-1/2} \log\log(λ)^{-1/2}$ if $d=3$ and $c(g) λ^{-1/2} \log(λ)^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}$ if $d >3$. This ball is centered at any point at which the eigenfunction attains its maximum in absolute value within the nodal domain. Furthermore, we show that for any $d \geq 3$, there exist sequences of $λ$-nodal domains on $\mathbb{T}^d$ whose inner radius is of order $o(λ^{-1/2})$.

</details>


### [129] [Coarse nodal counts on sub-Riemannian manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01446)
*Irene Silvestre-Roselló,Vukašin Stojisavljević*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: This paper proves coarse (persistent-homology-based) versions of Courant's and Bézout's theorems for nodal sets of linear combinations of eigenfunctions of sub-Laplacians on compact nilmanifolds (quotients of stratified groups), and conjectures extensions to general closed equiregular sub-Riemannian manifolds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize recent results on coarse topology of nodal sets from the Riemannian setting to the sub-Riemannian setting, where eigenfunctions of sub-Laplacians have different regularity and geometric properties.

Method: Combines topological persistence with anisotropic Sobolev theory of Hörmander vector fields, generalizing techniques previously implemented in the Riemannian case.

Result: Proves coarse versions of Courant's and Bézout's theorems for linear combinations of eigenfunctions of sub-Laplacians on compact nilmanifolds obtained as quotients of stratified groups.

Conclusion: Conjectures extensions of these results to general closed equiregular sub-Riemannian manifolds and outlines a programme for proving them.

Abstract: We study coarse topology of nodal sets of linear combinations of eigenfunctions of sub-Laplacians. More precisely, we prove coarse versions of Courant's and Bézout's theorems for linear combinations of eigenfunctions of sub-Laplacians on compact nilmanifolds obtained as quotients of stratified groups. We conjecture the extensions of these results to general closed equiregular sub-Riemannian manifolds and outline a programme for proving them. The method we use combines topological persistence and anisotropic Sobolev theory of Hörmander vector fields, generalizing the ideas which have recently been implemented in the Riemannian case.

</details>


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [130] [Multiscale passive scalar turbulence in a compressed subspace via tensor trains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00194)
*Stefano Pisoni,Egor Tiunov,Chiara Calascibetta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper introduces a hybrid Tensor Train (TT) approach for reduced-order modeling of intermittent passive scalars in turbulence, which matches established decompositions for structure functions while improving representation of non-Gaussian fluctuations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capturing the multiscale statistics of turbulence in compressed form is a central challenge for reduced-order modeling, particularly for highly intermittent passive scalars.

Method: A hybrid Tensor Train (TT) approach is developed that combines elements of Galerkin, wavelet, and standard TT decompositions to represent intermittent, non-Gaussian fluctuations in passive scalar fields.

Result: The hybrid TT matches Galerkin, wavelet, and standard TT decompositions for structure functions while improving the representation of intermittent, non-Gaussian fluctuations.

Conclusion: The approach opens a route toward evolving linear dynamics of passive scalars directly in compressed tensor form, with potential applications to quantum algorithms for fluid transport.

Abstract: Capturing the multiscale statistics of turbulence in compressed form remains a central challenge for reduced-order modeling. We introduce a hybrid Tensor Train (TT) approach for a highly intermittent passive scalar. The hybrid TT matches Galerkin, wavelet, and standard TT decompositions for the structure functions while improving the representation of intermittent, non-Gaussian fluctuations. These results open a route toward evolving the linear dynamics of passive scalars directly in compressed tensor form, with potential applications to quantum algorithms for fluid transport.

</details>


### [131] [Physics-informed neural networks for two-dimensional wall-reactive solute dispersion in canonical shear flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00856)
*Nanda Poddar,Subham Dhar*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper introduces a physics-informed neural network (PINN) framework to simulate two-dimensional wall-reactive solute dispersion in canonical shear flows (Couette, Poiseuille, Couette-Poiseuille) bounded by absorbing walls, validated against an ADI finite-difference benchmark, and used to extract transport diagnostics such as axial dispersion coefficients and wall uptake fluxes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately resolving spatiotemporal solute plume evolution in asymmetric reactive environments remains computationally demanding with classical analytical methods and grid-based solvers.

Method: A mesh-free PINN embeds the governing convection-diffusion equation and Robin boundary conditions into a unified loss function to reconstruct the spatiotemporal concentration field, tested at Pe=10 across non-reactive, symmetric, and asymmetric reactive wall conditions.

Result: PINN predictions closely match the ADI finite-difference benchmark. Results show that shear profile governs streamwise reactive uptake organization, while unequal wall reactivities induce transverse asymmetry that modifies macroscopic spreading rates.

Conclusion: PINNs are established as an interpretable mesh-free tool for analyzing boundary-coupled reactive transport in shear flows, enabling extraction of wall-resolved transport diagnostics from the differentiable network.

Abstract: The dispersion of reactive solutes in shear flows is governed by the interplay between advective stretching, transverse diffusion, and boundary exchange kinetics. While classical analytical methods and grid-based numerical solvers have extensively characterised these transport mechanisms, accurately resolving the spatiotemporal evolution of solute plumes in asymmetric reactive environments remains computationally demanding. In this study, we introduce a physics-informed neural network (PINN) framework to simulate two-dimensional wall-reactive solute dispersion in canonical shear flows (Couette, Poiseuille, and Couette-Poiseuille) bounded by absorbing walls. By embedding the governing convection-diffusion equation and Robin boundary conditions into a unified loss function, the mesh-free PINN reconstructs the spatiotemporal concentration field. The network predictions are validated against an alternating-direction implicit (ADI) finite-difference benchmark, showing close agreement across non-reactive, symmetric, and asymmetric reactive regimes. The computations are carried out at $\mathrm{Pe}=10$ for impermeable walls, symmetric absorption $(β_1,β_2)=(1,1)$, and tenfold asymmetric wall-reactivity contrasts $(β_1,β_2)=(0.2,2)$ and (2,0.2). Leveraging the differentiable nature of the trained PINN, we extract wall-resolved transport diagnostics, including the apparent axial dispersion coefficient, cumulative wall-removal dynamics, and localised uptake fluxes. The results show that the imposed shear profile governs the streamwise organisation of reactive uptake, while unequal wall reactivities induce transverse asymmetry that modifies the macroscopic spreading rate. Overall, this framework establishes PINNs as an interpretable mesh-free tool for analysing boundary-coupled reactive transport in shear flows.

</details>


### [132] [Supersonic jet impingement on concave surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01542)
*Hemanth Chandravamsi,Dhanush Vittal Shenoy,Steven H. Frankel*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This paper uses large-eddy simulations, vortex-sheet modelling, and Powell's feedback-loop analysis to investigate aeroacoustic resonance of supersonic jets impinging on concave surfaces, finding that narrower concave indentations amplify screech tones by up to 23 dB due to acoustic focusing and increased feedback efficiency. Helical tones are governed by guided jet modes from the shear-layer profile, while axisymmetric tones follow Powell's loop-length criterion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how wall curvature affects aeroacoustic resonance (screech) in supersonic impinging jets, and to identify the frequency-selection mechanisms governing different screech modes, as this has implications for controlling noise and surface loading in engineering applications.

Method: Compressible large-eddy simulations of ideally expanded Mach 1.56 jets (Re=6e4) impinging on two flat plates and four Gaussian concave surfaces with varying indentation spreads (σ = 0.4-4.0), combined with vortex-sheet modelling for guided-mode prediction and Powell-Tam source-transfer budget analysis for feedback quantification.

Result: Narrower concave indentations increase primary-tone amplitude by up to 23 dB over flat-wall reference, with increased wall-pressure fluctuations. The amplification is attributed to increased Mach-disk source amplitude and more efficient upstream feedback wave return due to acoustic focusing. Helical frequencies and radial eigenfunctions match vortex-sheet guided jet modes (independent of wall geometry), while axisymmetric frequencies follow Powell's classical loop-length criterion instead.

Conclusion: Distinct frequency-selection mechanisms exist for helical and axisymmetric screech: helical tones are governed by the shear-layer profile of the equivalent ideally expanded jet, while axisymmetric tones follow the classical loop-length criterion. Wall curvature provides effective control of screech amplitude and surface loading through acoustic focusing effects.

Abstract: The aeroacoustic resonance of round supersonic jets impinging on concave surfaces is investigated using compressible large-eddy simulations, vortex-sheet modelling, and Powell's feedback-loop analysis. The choked jets operate at an ideally expanded Mach number of $1.56$ and a Reynolds number of $6\times10^4$. Six geometries are considered: two flat plates at $L/D=2.08$ and $2.58$, where $L$ is the nozzle-to-wall distance and $D$ the nozzle exit diameter, and four Gaussian concave surfaces of fixed depth and indentation spread $σ\in\{0.4,0.8,1.6,4.0\}$. As the indentation narrows, the primary-tone amplitude increases by up to $23\,\mathrm{dB}$ relative to the flat-wall reference at $L/D=2.6$, together with larger wall-pressure fluctuations and moments. A Powell-Tam source-transfer budget attributes this amplification to increased Mach-disk source amplitude and more efficient return of the upstream feedback wave to the nozzle. The stronger upstream-propagating waves are consistent with acoustic focusing by the concave wall. For the helical cases, the measured frequencies and radial eigenfunctions agree closely with the guided jet mode predicted by the vortex-sheet model, supporting its role in closing the upstream feedback path. The same selection is recovered for concave and flat walls alike, so this tone is governed by the shear-layer profile of the equivalent ideally expanded jet rather than by the wall geometry. The axisymmetric frequencies, by contrast, coincide with no guided-mode branch and appear instead to follow Powell's classical loop-length criterion. The results identify distinct frequency-selection mechanisms for helical and axisymmetric screech and demonstrate that wall curvature provides effective control of screech amplitude and surface loading.

</details>


### [133] [Aerodynamic Drag and Heat Transfer Corrections for Dehydrated Pollen Particles: CFD-Based Modeling of Airborne Allergen Transport in Smart Urban Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02360)
*Omar Hamad,Samer Ali,Mahmoud Khaled,Talib Dbouk*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This study presents the first advanced CFD simulations of aerodynamic drag and convective heat transfer for realistically dehydrated (dry) pollen particles at Reynolds numbers 0.1-15, finding that dry pollen has 8-15% higher drag coefficients and 5-15% lower Nusselt numbers compared to hydrated spherical particles, demonstrating that conventional spherical correlations are inadequate for modeling airborne pollen transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional dispersion models assume smooth spherical pollen particles, neglecting how dehydration alters pollen morphology and affects aerodynamic and thermal behavior, which is critical for urban air-quality assessment, allergy-risk forecasting, and smart-city planning.

Method: Advanced CFD simulations evaluating aerodynamic drag forces and convective heat transfer of realistically dehydrated (dry) pollen particles at the particle scale, covering Reynolds numbers from 0.1 to 15, corresponding to atmospheric wind speeds of 0.27 to 30 km/h.

Result: Dry pollen particles exhibit drag coefficients 8-15% higher than hydrated spherical pollen predictions, while their Nusselt numbers are 5-15% lower than hydrated pollen particles, showing significant deviations from conventional spherical correlations.

Conclusion: Conventional spherical correlations are inadequate for simulating dry pollen Lagrangian transport and evaporation, highlighting the need to account for realistic dehydrated pollen shapes when modeling airborne allergen transport in urban environments.

Abstract: Airborne pollen transport is a key concern for urban air-quality assessment, allergy-risk forecasting, and smart-city planning. However, conventional dispersion models generally assume smooth spherical particles, neglecting how pollen dehydration alters particle morphology and impacts aerodynamic and thermal behavior. To address this gap, this study presents, for the first time, advanced CFD simulations evaluating the aerodynamic drag forces and convective heat transfer of realistically dehydrated (dry) pollen particles. Investigations are conducted at Reynolds numbers ($0.1 \leq Re_p \leq 15$) at the particle's scale corresponding to realistic atmospheric wind speeds ranging from 0.27 to 30 km/h. The findings reveal that dry pollen particles exhibit drag coefficients 8% to 15% higher than those predicted for hydrated pollen spherical particles. Conversely, their Nusselt numbers are 5% to 15% lower than those for hydrated pollen particles. These considerable deviations confirm that conventional spherical correlations are inadequate for simulating dry pollen Lagrangian transport and evaporation. These findings highlight the need to account for realistic dehydrated shapes when modeling airborne allergen transport in urban environments.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [134] [A Physics-Chemistry-Informed Neural Network (PCINN) for Real-Time Spatial-ALD Coverage Prediction and Reliable Kinetics Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00212)
*Ning Hu,Chang Liu,Yunlei Jiang,Yuan Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a physics-chemistry-informed neural network (PCINN) for spatial atomic layer deposition (SALD) that achieves CFD-level accuracy at ~7ms per query (5×10⁴× faster than CFD), with R²_log = 0.998 from only 30 training cases. The architecture embeds known surface kinetics as a hard-coded trainable layer, keeping it interpretable and invertible. A full identifiability analysis shows E_ads and k_des are robustly identifiable, while k_ads is only identifiable as k_ads·c_wall; a degeneracy valley slope of 0.065 eV/decade serves as a diagnostic for unmodelled site heterogeneity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SALD is a leading industrial ALD route but design/control is limited by the cost of predicting surface coverage: high-fidelity CFD is too slow for operating-window scans, while analytic models miss transport modulation such as the gas curtain. A fast yet accurate surrogate is needed.

Method: A hybrid PCINN where a small neural network learns only the operating-condition-to-near-wall concentration closure, while known surface kinetics are a hard-coded, trainable chemistry layer integrated along the substrate trajectory. A single-scalar bottleneck maintains accuracy under sparse data, interpretability, and invertibility. Identifiability is assessed via Fisher information and profile likelihood, plus a seven-chemistry mismatch matrix diagnostic.

Result: The PCINN achieves ~7ms per query (~5×10⁴× faster than CFD) with test R²_log = 0.998 and leave-one-out R²_raw = 0.974 from 30 training cases spanning four orders of magnitude in coverage. E_ads and k_des are robustly identifiable; k_ads is not separately identifiable at a single temperature. A degeneracy valley of slope 0.065 eV/decade is invariant under single-Arrhenius mismatch and shifts only with a second thermally activated process.

Conclusion: The study verifies pipeline self-consistency and the identifiability boundary using simulated data with known ground truth. The slope departure from 0.065 eV/decade serves as a diagnostic flag for unmodelled site heterogeneity. Real parameter estimation from experimental data is left for future work.

Abstract: Spatial atomic layer deposition (SALD) is a leading atmospheric-pressure, high-throughput route to industrial ALD, but design and control are limited by the cost of predicting surface coverage: high-fidelity CFD is far too slow for operating-window scans, while analytic models miss transport modulation such as the gas curtain. We present a physics-chemistry-informed neural network (PCINN), a hybrid surrogate with CFD-level accuracy at real-time speed: a query returns coverage in about 7 ms, roughly 5x10^4 times faster than a CFD solve, reaching a test R^2_log = 0.998 (leave-one-out R^2_raw = 0.974) from only 30 training cases spanning four orders of magnitude in coverage.
  The architecture is not a black box: a small network learns only the operating-condition to near-wall concentration closure, while the known surface kinetics is a hard-coded, trainable chemistry layer integrated along the substrate trajectory. This single-scalar bottleneck keeps it accurate under sparse data, interpretable and invertible.
  We add a full identifiability analysis (Fisher information, profile likelihood). The adsorption energy E_ads and desorption rate k_des are robustly identifiable; k_ads is not separately identifiable at a single temperature (only k_ads*c_wall is). Across four temperatures the prefactor nu and E_ads bind along a weakly identifiable degeneracy valley of slope 0.065 eV/decade, derived analytically as k_B T_eff ln(10) and turned into a reliability diagnostic: a seven-chemistry mismatch matrix shows it is invariant under any single-Arrhenius mismatch and shifts only when a second thermally activated process appears, so a slope departure flags unmodelled site heterogeneity.
  Data come from simulation with known ground truth inverted by the same kinetic form, so the study verifies pipeline self-consistency and the identifiability boundary, not real parameters.

</details>


### [135] [Modeling Unknown Nonlocal PDE Systems via Flow Map Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00400)
*Zhongshu Xu,Ying Li,Yanzhi Zhang,Dongbin Xiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A flow-map learning (FML) framework for modeling unknown nonlocal PDEs directly from solution data, learning finite-time evolution operators in modal or nodal space instead of approximating nonlocal operators, demonstrated on fractional diffusion and wave equations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlocal PDEs are difficult to model and learn due to the presence of nonlocal operators, and existing approaches that attempt to approximate these operators directly face significant challenges.

Method: Learn the finite-time evolution operator (flow map) from solution data rather than the underlying nonlocal operators, using two complementary formulations for spectral (modal) and grid-based (nodal) solution representations.

Result: Numerical experiments on 1D and 2D fractional diffusion and wave equations show accurate and stable long-time predictions using only short observation windows.

Conclusion: The FML approach provides an effective data-driven framework for learning unknown nonlocal dynamics without explicit evaluation of nonlocal operators.

Abstract: Nonlocal partial differential equations arise in many applications but are often difficult to model and learn because of the presence of nonlocal operators. We present a flow-map learning (FML) framework for modeling unknown nonlocal PDEs directly from solution data. Rather than learning or approximating the underlying nonlocal operators, the proposed approach learns the finite-time evolution operator in either modal or nodal space. Two complementary formulations are developed for spectral and grid-based solution representations. Numerical experiments on one- and two-dimensional fractional diffusion and wave equations demonstrate accurate and stable long-time prediction using only short observation windows. The proposed approach provides an effective data-driven framework for learning unknown nonlocal dynamics without explicit evaluation of nonlocal operators.

</details>


### [136] [tFUSOperator: Operator Learning for Transcranial Focused Ultrasound Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01839)
*Minjee Seo,Haris Ghafoor,Minju Seol,Seonaeng Cho,Kyungho Yoon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes tFUSOperator, a coordinate-aware neural operator for transcranial focused ultrasound (tFUS) simulation that maps free-field pressure, skull anatomy, and treatment parameters to the intracranial acoustic field in a shared physical coordinate frame—the first operator-based formulation of tFUS field prediction. It achieves ~90% and ~72% Dice on seen and unseen skulls respectively, performs nearly as well from MR as from CT input, and runs 5.6×10⁴ times faster than numerical simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate intracranial acoustic field estimation for tFUS is distorted by skull-induced aberrations. Numerical solvers are accurate but computationally expensive for digital twins requiring repeated field estimation. Existing deep-learning surrogates are fast but use fixed-grid voxel-to-voxel regression without mechanisms reflecting acoustic energy propagation through the skull, motivating a better surrogate approach.

Method: The authors cast tFUS simulation as an operator learning problem and propose tFUSOperator, a coordinate-aware neural operator that maps free-field pressure, skull anatomy, and treatment parameters to the intracranial field within a shared physical coordinate frame, explicitly incorporating the physics of acoustic propagation.

Result: The model localizes the acoustic focus accurately on both seen and unseen skulls, reaching approximately 90% and 72% Dice respectively. It performs nearly as well from MR input as from CT and runs 5.6×10⁴ times faster than numerical simulation.

Conclusion: The results suggest a fast, radiation-free route to safe and practical digital twins for patient-specific tFUS treatment.

Abstract: Transcranial focused ultrasound (tFUS) requires accurate estimation of the intracranial acoustic field, which is distorted by skull-induced aberrations. Numerical solvers are accurate but computationally expensive for digital twins, where the field must be re-estimated repeatedly as treatment conditions change. Existing deep-learning surrogates are fast but typically use voxel-to-voxel regression on a fixed grid, with no mechanism reflecting how acoustic energy propagates through the skull. We instead cast tFUS simulation as an operator learning problem and propose tFUSOperator, a coordinate-aware neural operator that maps the free-field pressure, skull anatomy, and treatment parameters to the intracranial field within a shared physical coordinate frame. To our knowledge, this is the first operator-based formulation of tFUS field prediction. On both seen and unseen skulls, the model localizes the acoustic focus accurately-reaching about 90% and 72% Dice, respectively-and it performs nearly as well from magnetic resonance (MR) as from computed tomography (CT) input while running $5.6 \times 10^4$ times faster than numerical simulation. These results suggest a fast, radiation-free route to safe and practical digital twins for patient-specific tFUS treatment. The code is available at: https://github.com/CMME-Lab/tFUSOperator.git.

</details>

### [137] [Wasserstein mixing time of the unadjusted Langevin algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02430)
*Francesco Pedrotti,Peter A. Whalley*

Main category: stat.CO

TL;DR: This paper provides improved Wasserstein distance estimates for the asymptotic bias of the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA) on log-smooth strongly log-concave measures, achieving a Wasserstein mixing time of order κ√d/ε, improving over prior state-of-the-art results by a factor of √d/ε.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA) is a fundamental sampling method, but prior bounds on its Wasserstein mixing time were suboptimal. The authors aim to tighten the theoretical guarantees for ULA's asymptotic bias in the standard setting of strongly log-concave targets, where previous results had a larger dimension and precision dependence.

Method: The paper develops new analytical techniques to bound the Wasserstein distance between the law of the ULA iterates and the target distribution. By refining the error propagation analysis of the discretized Langevin dynamics, the authors obtain sharper dimension and precision dependence in the asymptotic bias estimates.

Result: The authors prove a Wasserstein mixing time of order κ√d/ε for ULA, which improves the previous state-of-the-art bound by a factor of √d/ε. This means the new bound has both a square-root dependence on the dimension and a 1/ε dependence on the target precision, instead of the earlier (worse) rates.

Conclusion: The improved Wasserstein estimates significantly tighten the theoretical guarantees for ULA on strongly log-concave targets, bringing the analysis closer to optimal and potentially closing the gap between theory and practice for this widely used sampling algorithm.

Abstract: We provide new estimates in Wasserstein distance for the asymptotic bias of the unadjusted Langevin algorithm, in the classical setting of log-smooth strongly log-concave measures. Our bound implies a Wasserstein mixing time of order $κ\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon$, where $κ$ is the condition number, $d$ is the dimension, and $\varepsilon$ is the target precision: this improves by a factor of $\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon$ over the previous state-of-the-art results.

</details>
